The local array is `checkSockets`' `dead`, the one shape added for today's code: a `const dead = []` of the method's own body, whose only other uses are `dead.push(socket)` inside the `this.sockets.forEach` callback whose parameter is `socket`, and `dead.forEach(…)`; it holds nothing but what that parameter holds, which the same check covers, and its `forEach` callbacks are arrows written there with one plain parameter (no index or array parameter, no `arguments`, no callback passed by name), and it is not read or written by index, assigned, spread, passed or called by another method. `new Socket(…)` and a for…of over the connections, on the list before, are off it: no unit that sends on the name binds it so (`onConnection` makes a `Socket` and sends nothing on it). (After the second review, a chain of local names was followed instead, and a name shadowed in a nested function still let a transport through; after the third, a binding was looked up by its scope, and a bare loop target, a function declaration of the name, a write in a parameter default, an assignment in a nested function and `this.sockets` read in a nested function let one through.) 66 counterexamples are refused in the guard's own tests and 7 shapes the network's code has are accepted. A test rewrites every method (62) and function-valued field (33) of today's class as a top-level function of a network module, the shape the moves will give them, and expects no violation, so that the guard's own false alarms are found before a move. Everywhere, a `send` or `ping` referred to without a call (extracted, bound, `.call`), taken out of an object (`{ send }`), a transport handed to a function as its `this` (`f.call(ws)`), and a computed key that is a string or a template without substitutions count. What each tripwire still cannot see is written at the top of it: for `serverWrites`, a transport handed in as an argument where an allowed parameter takes it, or put into the network's own connections; a member of the class or a module's function called as a method of something else, and another class written in one of the network's files; a key computed at run time; a helper that takes the transport; files it does not read (a transport kept in a property is refused: `this.raw.send` is not an allowed receiver, and `socket = this.raw` not an allowed origin); for `imageCheck`, a function the named ones call and what it is handed, a logger reached through another module or a call outside the inspected functions and called there by a plain name, a logging member of another object reached or destructured by a key computed at run time (`audit[level](…)`, `const { [level]: f } = audit`), other ways of writing (`process.stderr.write`, `process.emitWarning`), and what a line says beyond its call's text. It already read `src/server` with its subfolders; it now also checks that every file of the network is read, and two tests build a checkout of their own (`networkSource.mjs` takes one as an argument) with a network module two folders down: it is found and its raw send refused, and a function moved there has its new log line refused.
  - **The tripwires, tried by hand.** 13 changes of the sources, each run against the old and the new file. A new log line in `saveDirty`, a second forbidden line in `flushBeforeExit`, `saveConfig` renamed, its failure line reworded, `userRows`' failure line taken out, a raw `ws.send` in the class, raw calls in modules of `src/server/network/` (one a folder deeper), `this.send` in another server file: both fail. `saveConfig` moved verbatim into `src/server/network/config.js`, with a member of the class that hands the call on: the new file passes, the old one failed (it looked for the function's text in `ServerNetwork.js`); the same move with a new unsorted line: both fail. `this.send` and `socket.send` in a module of `src/server/network/`: the new file passes, the old one failed; the same in another file named `ServerNetwork.js`: the new file fails, the old one passed. After the review, nine more, each against the files before and after that round: in the stop path, a log call with a space before its parenthesis (`saveDirty`), one through a renamed console (`flushBeforeExit`), one through a destructured logger (`saveConfig`) and a `console.trace` (`userRows`); in the class, a send taken out of the transport (`const { send } = ws`), one with a template key, one through an alias named `socket`, one from a nested function's `this`, and a network module that hands a transport to a function as its `this`. Each passed before and fails now. After the second review, eight more passed and fail now: a send through a chain of two local names, of three, through a destructured alias (`const { ws: raw } = …`), a ping through a reassigned alias in a network module; and a stop-path log line written through `.call` (`saveDirty`), `.apply` (`flushBeforeExit`), `Reflect.apply` (`saveConfig`) and a `.bind(…)` called at once (`userRows`). After the third review, six more: Codex's two (a transport given to `socket` through a local name whose initializer a nested function shadows, in a network module) and four variants (the same in the class, a connection given another value later, a member of something else under an allowed name, and a condition between a connection and a transport, which was refused before as well); five passed before and fail now. For the loggers, eleven more, inserted into the stop path's own functions: Codex's two (a logger given a second local name in `saveDirty`, and one assigned later) with two variants each, three through `installShutdown`'s `log` (taken out, in a chain, a computed member), a computed member of `console` and another method of it called directly; each fails now (how many passed before depends on how the computed members are written, `console[level]` or `console['warn']`, and is not counted here). After the fourth review, twenty-one more, each against the files before (`681a013`) and after that round. In the class's `write` (two in a network module of their own): a bare loop target (Codex's), one of a for…in and one destructured, a function declaration named `socket`, a write in a parameter default, an assignment in a nested function under a raw first value (Codex's), `this.sockets.get(…)` in a nested function given another `this` (Codex's), `this.sockets.forEach` there and an object method's `this.sockets.get(…)`, and the two shapes taken off the list (a for…of over the connections, `new Socket(…)`): these eleven passed before and fail now; a raw value written by a nested function, a binding at a module's top level and a local array that receives another name were refused before as well. In `saveDirty`: `this.logOnce` kept in two names (Codex's), `this.audit.warn` handed to another function and a destructured `error` kept in another name passed before and fail now; `this.logOnce` destructured or bound, `this.audit.error` taken out and a nested function's `{ log = console }` option taken out failed before as well. After the fifth review, thirteen more, each against the files before (`fbaa5b3`) and after that round, inserted into today's bodies. In `saveDirty`: a nested function's option calling `log.table` (Codex's), the same in a nested arrow, a renamed option (`{ log: audit = console }`), a logging member destructured by a template key from another object (Codex's) and `logOnce` from `this`, and `console`'s `warn` destructured by a template key at the module's top and called there by its new name. In `checkSockets`: `dead`'s callback with its index and array writing a transport into it (Codex's), a `function` callback doing so through `arguments`, a callback passed by name, each followed by a send to every element; and `socket()` in `onPing` (Codex's), `tSocket()` in `onKick`, `this()` in `write`.   - **Removed.** `test/lib/networkHarness.mjs`: its list of 90 method names and their cutter, the names injected in place of the class's imports, the `Socket` cut out of `Socket.js`, its `makeNetwork`, and `loadRoute` (now in `wsRoute.mjs`). From `persistenceHarness.mjs`: `Settings` and `Entities` (no test used them), the export of `source`, and of what `load` gave, every name but those the files it still evaluates import from the project's modules (given one by one). `networkSource.mjs` exports only what the two tripwires import. `load` stays for `cleaner`, `cleanerTrash`, `collections` and `persistence` (`ServerAI`): classes that are not the network, still evaluated from their text.
  - **Checks the class caught.** An assertion a test makes in code the network calls and catches is taken by the class for a failure of its own (logged, or worked around), and the test goes on. The assertions that run while the class is on the stack, in the files moved to the real class, were looked for: 33 places by running those files with every such assertion recorded, and 4 more by reading (reached through `ServerAI`, or in a test the recording did not see). Each was made to fail, one at a time, and the tests that reached it were looked at (for `inboundNetwork`'s handler a mutant had shown it already). Three places let a test pass that had reached the failed check. `inboundNetwork`'s test of a close during a flush asserted inside the handler the flush dispatched: what it sees is now recorded and asserted after the flush, with nothing logged, and it catches an `onDisconnect` that leaves the closed connection's credit in the shared pool until the next flush, where the scheduler would purge it (before, nothing did). `connectionLifecycle`'s world events (`enter`), inside `onConnection`'s catch, which closes the join: 4 of the 15 tests that reach them passed. `fakeWs`'s check of a ping (no payload, unmasked), inside the write ledger's catch, which takes a throwing ping for a dead connection: 7 of 12 passed. `test/lib/network.mjs` has `inside(t, check)`: what a check throws is thrown on as before and reported again at the end of the test that holds the helper; `fakeWs` and `connectionLifecycle`'s collaborators (all of them) check with it, and two `networkHelper` tests try it. The others were noticed by every test that reached them.
  - **After the review: every check outside a test's own body.** The search above saw only assertions that ran while the class was on the stack, and missed checks that fire only when the product misbehaves: the getters of a thrown value that `networkLogging` and `connectionLifecycle` give, which `briefError` reads inside a catch of its own (a `briefError` that read `err.message` left every logging test green), and the token balance `networkLogging` must not read. It took in, next, every assertion of the moved files and their helpers that is not in a test's own callback (100 places), each made to fail in turn. 70 were noticed by every test that reached them; one more let its test pass: `sharedObjects`' check that an app's touch facade cannot be redefined, made in a control's callback, which the controls catch (it is now recorded there and asserted after). 29 were reached by no passing test: traps of that kind. Each that a catch could hide now goes through `inside()` (`networkLogging`'s thrown value, packet, balance read and console; `connectionLifecycle`'s thrown value; the disabled handlers' connection in `serverWallet` and `serverEvmWallet`; `networkTransforms`' failed join) or is recorded and asserted after the act (`adminCommand`'s command event, `physicsFailure`'s fatal close, `scriptChat`'s player lookup, `writeLedger`'s send).
  - **`inside()`, after the review.** A check that fails while the test's end stops the networks is reported by that end (the end used to drop it). One that fails after the end has reported (in a later hook), or for a test that never made a network, is thrown outside every catch of the class from a turn of its own, scheduled from the async context the helper was loaded in, outside every test: node:test reports it as an uncaught exception of the file and charges no test with it. (Scheduled from the caller's context at first, it failed whatever test that context belonged to: an earlier test's connection pinged in a later test failed the later one, and so did a connection made at the file's top or in a `before` hook, in one file or across files run in one process.) A fake connection belongs to the test that held the helper when it was made, not to the one running when it is pinged. A check must be synchronous: what it returns is read inside the report as well (a `then` getter that fails is reported), one that returns a thenable is refused with a `TypeError`, reported the same way, and a rejection of it is handled, so that it is not an unhandled rejection besides. A failure that nothing catches on its way is reported twice, where it is thrown and again (by the end, or as an uncaught exception): telling the two apart would take knowing whether the test saw it. What `inside()` does not do is written in the helper's header. Five `networkHelper` tests; one runs four test files in a child `node --test` (with its reporter named), the later tests of which wait until the throw has come, and expects each file to fail on an uncaught exception while every test of it passes.
  - **Mutants.** 130 mutants of the product code these files guard (31 from the earlier mutation scripts, 99 new: the route's version check, kick and close; the admission's lease; four of the six ws options; the inbound copy, queue, flush and dispatch; the refusal lines and their keys; the stop check of the flush and of each handler; the packet registry and the handler inventory), each counted for the one file that guards it. Per file, its mutants and how many that file catches: `protocolVersion` 11 and 11, `wsAdmission` 14 and 12, `wsTransport` 11 and 11 (its eight cases ran with real TCP), `inboundNetwork` 18 and 17, `networkLogging` 14 and 14, `physicsFailure` 24 and 24, `packetAccess` 10 and 10, `connectionLifecycle` 28 and 12 (the place a join holds and what a departed connection held, taken from an earlier script written for `connectionHolds` as well): 111 of the 130 (the files as they were before this pull request caught 108). Of the 19 each guarding file misses, another file of the suite catches 18: the 16 of `connectionLifecycle` by `connectionHolds`, `wsAdmission`'s transport without its error listener by `wsTransport`, and `inboundNetwork`'s gate whose budget never refills by `inboundPolicy`, `eventBudget` and `playerCoalescing` (and `networkHelper`, whose run of the moved files includes those, by the same). One is caught by no file: `wsAdmission`'s lease released on every `close` instead of once; the release does nothing the second time, so the two differ only in a listener left on a closed transport. (Counted again after the first review, against the files as of `78755de`.)

What a program can see differently is `Object.prototype.toString` of those four members (`[object Function]`, not `[object AsyncFunction]`), which nothing reads. Every one caught before is caught after, by the same tests; the 29 caught only after are caught by tests this pull request added (the timers' `networkHelper` test and the next sub-bullet's), and the same tests catch them on the old code (through that hook: 70 of 73, the same verdicts but the stop path's tripwire, which reads this checkout's source). The two left change no row that is written (next sub-bullet). One verdict before this pull request's tests depended on the machine's load: the config writes not queued were caught only by `shutdownSave`'s test of queued config writes, which caught them in two of four runs of the baseline with the other files, in none of four of the moved code's, and alone in every run (next sub-bullet: why, and its fix). The e2e suites `access-control` (no browser), `fullstack` and `gate-extras` (uploads, builder saves) pass on Chromium and WebKit, and their servers' logs show the moved code at work (twelve hydrations, twelve final saves complete, `world saved` lines, nothing `not persisted`); none of the e2e suites restarts a server on its saved world (`persistedStartup`, `persistenceIntegration` and `shutdownSave` do, in the suite). Test comments follow the move (`network.mjs`, `networkSource.mjs`, `networkTimers.mjs`, `serverBundle.mjs`).
  - **What no test caught, now tested.** Of the fourth seam's mutants, 23 were caught by no test before the move or after it (24 with one added after the review: the collections counted at start), and one only by a test whose result depended on the load. Each that changes what the server does now fails a test that says the rule, in `test/persistenceRules.test.js` (the real class; a world that starts runs the bundle's `Settings` and `Blueprints`, where the persistence fixture sets the loaded mark of the config rows and the settings' listener by hand, which is why a start that recorded neither was never seen) and `test/persistenceSqlite.test.js` (real SQLite): settings loaded at start are written back when they change, at the settings' tick, and a change made after the last tick by the final save; a config row that could not be read at start is never written over, and that is said once; a value equal to the stored one is not written again; a config row's writes go one at a time, and the newer value is the one stored; a newer value stays to be written when an older write of the row ends (the final save writes it after); a settings write that fails is not saved (the final save is incomplete and names the row); an app re-keyed and then deleted before the save leaves neither its old row nor its new one; a save that only removes apps says so in its line; a save that fails does not keep the next one from writing; while a queued save writes, the next save is not timed (it is timed once, at the end of the last save), and a final save started then waits for it; the save gives the event loop a turn before each row, the first included; no row and no config row of the final save starts once its deadline has passed by the clock, whether the clock passes it while a row is written or during the turn before the next row, nor when the clock is then set back (a thread held by synchronous writes runs the deadline's timer late; these tests give the network their own timers, so the deadline's timer is never run and only the mocked clock decides); a world stopped while it loads times no save; the start's line counts the apps that loaded, not the rows read; the settings and collections loaded at start (a collection there) count in the join state from the start; an app row written again keeps the time it was created. Each fails on its mutant, within its time limit of 30 s (60 s for SQLite: a budget for a loaded machine, not a bound on the test) where the mutant keeps a write it waits for from ever starting, and the tests wait for the write, save or start they are about, never for a number of turns; where one has the event loop run a callback of its own, it queues it before the code it watches can get there. Two mutants change no row that is written, with today's collaborators, and have no test. Without the final save's return when its deadline passed while it waited for a save under way, it still calls `save()` and the config savers after the deadline: each returns without writing a row, but the save and config queues get a new settled promise and the config row its dirty mark again. Without the release of the config queue, `network.configPromise` stays a settled promise where it was `null`: a write queued behind it starts as behind none, since that promise never rejects (each failure inside it resolves `false`; the save queue's release is different, a save can reject, and is tested). The early deadline checks of `saveDirty` and `saveConfig`, which this entry first counted with them, are tested: without them, a write found past the deadline before its turn of the event loop can still start when the clock is set back during that turn. `shutdownSave`'s test of queued config writes sent its signal at the child's `ready`, which the child prints before the writes it queued have started: when the child was slower than the test, the signal came first and set the deadline before any write began, and writes that were not queued then stopped at the deadline as well, each checking the clock, so the test passed them. Now the child's first write, once written, holds the rest until the child has taken the signal, and the test signals when the child says so: queued writes all start after the final save has its deadline, and stop at it; writes that were not queued were all waiting for one turn of the event loop, which runs them all, the held one aside, before the signal can be taken, and the final save then finds nothing left and exits 0. Correct code passed and that mutant failed in five runs each; neither verdict depends on how fast the child or the test runs (only the number of writes made before the deadline does, about ten). And for the review: a `sourceRoot` whose class does not import `persistence.js` loads, and the class's timers there are the test's (`networkHelper`); the persistence fixture hands `makeNetwork`'s `timers` on. The suite has 3,450 tests.
  - **Moved out, the fifth seam: authority.** Who may do what is in `src/server/network/authority.js`: the 222 lines of its eight members in `ServerNetwork.js` (lines 454-462, 598-620 and 1097-1291 at `d4ccff08`), each now a top-level function whose `this` is the network, its body as it was (every line two spaces less indented, the same comments and blank lines): the builder check the builder handlers ask for (`requireBuilder`), the default avatar and what a player who stops being a builder keeps (`defaultAvatar`, `dropBuilderUrls`), the chat commands (`onCommand`: `/admin` against the admin code and its brake, `/name`, `/spawn`, `/chat clear`, `/server stats`, the others handed to server scripts), a change of rank (`onModifyRank`), and kick and mute with the rule they share (`moderated`, `onKick`, `onMute`). The module-local `isObject` the handlers use is written again there, as `solanaWallet.js` has it. This splits the file along a responsibility; it does not decouple anything: the functions read and write the network's state as before (the admin mode and its brake, the kick list, the action budgets, the users' rows) and call the class's `spend`, `send`, `storeRank`, `userRows`, `onSpawnModified`, `admin` and `kicks` through the network. Each member stays declared in the class where it was, under its name and of its kind (four methods, and the four handlers as arrow fields), with the same parameters, and hands its call on with the same arguments (`onKick = (socket, playerId, meta) => authority.onKick.call(this, socket, playerId, meta)`; a method returns the same call): dispatch, the builder handlers (`requireBuilder`), the join (`defaultAvatar`), the token gate and the world's rank setting (`dropBuilderUrls`), and tests that replace a member find the same members. The two that were `async` (`onCommand`, `onModifyRank`) return the moved function's own promise. `ServerNetwork.js` no longer imports `chatCommand.js` (`Chat.js` imports it earlier in the server bundle, where it stays), and its one new import comes last. Two members of the planned seam stay in the class: the admin mode with the brake on admin codes (`admin`) and the kick list (`kicks`). Without them the class would no longer import `adminCode.js` and `kickList.js`, and each would move in the server bundle's order (tried in a scratch copy: from after `userRows.js` to after `persistence.js`), so they stay, and no import is kept only to hold a module's place. `requireBuilder`, which the world's handlers call, moved with the seam: it is the check of who may build. The file is 1,748 lines (1,942 before this step).
  - **Its evidence.** The eight bodies, found by parsing, are the same text once their indentation is removed, and so are their parameters, their `async`, the comments above them and the helper with its comment; each delegation is of its member's kind, with its parameters, and the class's members are in the same order. `git diff --color-moved=dimmed-zebra --color-moved-ws=allow-indentation-change` shows 212 removed and 212 added lines as moved; what is not is the import lines, the new module's head, imports and helper, the eight function heads, closing braces and blank lines, the four arrow-field heads the class changed, and the delegations with their comments. (With `--color-moved-ws=no` only 29 lines on each side show as moved, the fields of the system lines that a deeper copy shares with a shallower one: every moved line is two spaces less indented.) For each member, and for five that stay as controls (`admin`, `kicks`, `spend`, `storeRank`, `onSpawnModified`), `typeof`, own or prototype, its descriptor, `length`, `name`, whether it is one function on one network and on two, what a call gives with plain arguments and with arguments that throw when touched (a value, a sync throw, or a promise and the microtask turn it settles on), what it sends and the lines it logs are what they were, and so is the order of a network's own properties and of the prototype's names; `Object.prototype.toString` of the two async handlers is `[object Function]` now. The server bundle (`npm run build`) has the same module markers in the same order, with one more, the new module's, right before the last part of `ServerNetwork.js`, after `persistence.js`: everything it imports has been evaluated by then, and its own top level declares the helper and functions; outside `ServerNetwork.js` the bundle differs only in names esbuild numbered again (`isObject` in `appFields.js` and `gltfLimits.js`, `moment` in `db.js`). The tripwires pass unchanged: `serverWrites` (the moved bodies send on `this`, a parameter `socket`, and `tSocket` from `this.sockets.get`), `imageCheck`, and `packetAccess`'s inventory of the class's `on<Name>` members. No moved body calls a timer function, so `TIMED_FILES` is as it was. The test modules a test gives (`loadNetwork`'s stand-ins) reach the moved code: a probe saw a line of the moved `onCommand` carry the id of the fixture's `uuid` stand-in, and failed when the module was changed by hand to call another `uuid`. Test infrastructure: `networkHelper`'s two tests of a `sourceRoot` whose class does not import `tips.js` or `persistence.js` list the network modules such a bundle has, and both lists name `authority.js` now (as the merge of the fourth seam added `persistence.js` to the first); the comment of `test/lib/networkSource.mjs` names the module. The whole suite (3,461 tests) and `npm run check` pass; the seam's 46 test files (806 tests) pass on the new sources and on the old ones (`d4ccff08`'s), through the hook `test/lib/network.mjs` documents for a baseline run. 117 mutants of the seam's product code (next sub-bullet), each made in `ServerNetwork.js` before the move and where the code is after, each run against the files that guard it: before this step (`d4ccff08`'s code and its own tests) 93 are caught, after it (this pull request's code and tests) 112, and the same 112 by this pull request's tests on the old code (through that hook). Every one caught before is caught after, by the same tests; the 19 caught only after are caught by `test/authorityRules.test.js`, on the old code too. The e2e suites `access-control` (no browser), `fullstack` and `gate-extras` pass on Chromium and WebKit, and the servers' logs show the moved code at work (a kick's line with its minutes, the builder check's refusals).
  - **The authority code: what no test caught, now tested.** 117 mutants of the authority code (`ServerNetwork.js` before the move, `authority.js` after it, `admin` and `kicks` in the class both times): who may run each command and each branch of `/admin` (the code as sent, the brake's three keys, its answers, the socket it closes and why, the wait scale, the open and locked modes, the toggle, what is stored), a change of rank (who may make it, of whom, to what, the guard of a locked world, every refusal and what it says), the rule of kick and mute, a kick's list entry and its address rule, what the kicked connection is sent and when it is closed, a mute's value, what a former builder keeps (each of the three urls, a script's choices, the packet), the default avatar, the builder check's line, and every budget the handlers spend and what a refusal over it says. None of these handlers has a stop check of its own: the flush's stops them before they run. Before this step 93 were caught and 24 passed every test. Nineteen change what the server does, and each now fails a test that says the rule, in `test/authorityRules.test.js` (the real class, players joined through the real `onConnection`): the default avatar is the world's own, for a visitor who joins with an avatar from outside the world and for a builder who stops being one (two mutants); a player who has left is neither changed nor told about by what a former builder keeps; the brake's waits are shortened by `ADMIN_ATTEMPT_WAIT_SCALE` outside production and not in it (on a mocked clock); a command's answers are lines of the system (no sender, an id and a time of the server's) to the sender alone: the admin code's, a name's, both lines of the process figures, and a locked world's refusal of an admin's own lowering (seven mutants); an `/admin` toggle is counted in the moderation budget, with kicks, mutes and changes of rank (as `actionBudget.js` says), not in the commands'; `/spawn` is counted with the settings at the arrival of the packet that carried it; `/name` cuts a name to 64 characters, and one with nothing visible in it changes nothing and is not answered; a change of rank or a mute whose payload is not an object is dropped, the handler ending without a failure (two mutants); a rank is visitor, builder or admin and nothing else; a kick refused over the budget is not answered, as a mute is not, and the next refusal that is, is; a change of rank names a player only (an app's id changes nothing). Each has a limit of 30 s, and none depends on real time. Five change nothing with today's collaborators and have no test. The admin gate given `ADMIN_CODE` in every mode: a mode other than `code` means that `ADMIN_CODE` is empty or unset, and the gate is off for both; what changes is which of the two it is given. `/name` without its own assignment of the player's name before `modify()`: the server's player sets it in `modify`, before its `name` event (and so does the tests' player). A change of rank without its check of an empty player id: one lookup more, which finds nothing (no entity has an empty id: an app's is `ENTITY_ID`, a player's a user id). Kick and mute looking their target up among all entities, not the players: an app found that way is outranked by nobody, since its record has no rank and `Math.max(undefined, …)` is `NaN`, which no rank is above. And a kick's list entry made after the close instead of before it: the handler does both in one synchronous run, what the close runs (`onDisconnect`, at once with the tests' transport, later with the real one) does not read the list, and a join reads it only after an await. No mutant showed a check that does not do what its comment, the README or `docs/commands.md` says. The suite has 3,461 tests.
- docs: README and DOCKER.md describe the list of `PUBLIC_` names the page is handed. Both still described it by the prefix (README: every `PUBLIC_` variable but `PUBLIC_RPC_URL`), which stopped being so with the list (see Security).
- dependencies and CI (P14): what is installed in production, a weekly audit, and a start-up check from a production install.
  - **What moved.** esbuild 0.24.2 → 0.25.12 (build tool). `@fastify/static` 10.1.7 → 10.1.10 inside its declared range, which brings glob 13 → 14 and path-scurry 2 → 3 below it. `find-cache-dir` is removed (nothing imported it). `esbuild-plugin-polyfill-node` is now a dev dependency (only the build scripts use it), so esbuild is no longer counted as a production package. `ws` stays at 8.22.0. `npm audit --omit=dev` went from 13 packages (10 moderate, 3 high) to 9 (6 moderate, 3 high) with these moves, and to 4 (4 moderate, no high) once `@solana/spl-token` left the production install (below). The shipped scripts differ by under 1% in size; the source maps are larger (the client's by 7%, the WalletConnect chunk's by 31%), because esbuild 0.25 follows dependencies' own source maps to their original files. For `npm run dev` only: esbuild 0.25 deletes the output files when a rebuild fails (0.24 kept the previous bundle); what dev does then is in the next line.
  - **A build that fails, fails.** `npm run node-client:build`, and `npm run build` in its last part (the server's bundle), exited 0 when that bundle failed to build: the plugin that finishes it (`scripts/lib/finalizePhysxBuild.mjs`) ended the process before esbuild could report the error, so a syntax error in the server left no `build/index.js`, or an old one, and a green build step. (A failure in the client's or the particles' bundle, which `npm run build` makes first, already failed the script.) A failed build of that bundle now exits 1. In `npm run dev` a failed rebuild leaves the running server as it is and says so (a first build that fails starts none); it used to stop the server and start it again on the output of the failed build, which esbuild 0.25 deletes (the new one died at once, and came back when the error was fixed).
  - **Audit job.** `npm run audit:prod` (`scripts/audit.mjs`; `.github/workflows/audit.yml` runs it on Mondays, on demand and on pull requests that change `package.json`, `package-lock.json`, the allowlist, the script or the workflow itself) fails on a high or critical advisory in the production dependencies, lists lower ones without failing, and fails when it gets no usable report. It reads npm's JSON, not its exit code, and counts an advisory once however many packages inherit it. It does not look at dev dependencies or at whether a vulnerable function is called.
  - **Allowlist.** `audit-allowlist.json` holds decisions to tolerate a known advisory until a date; an entry fixes nothing. After its date the advisory fails the job again, and an entry that matches no reported high or critical advisory fails as stale. It is empty: the one high advisory of the production install, [GHSA-3gc7-fjrx-p6mg](https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-3gc7-fjrx-p6mg) (CVE-2025-3194), a buffer overflow in `bigint-buffer`'s `toBigIntLE()` that can crash the process, is no longer in it (next item). Four moderate advisories (stream-json, uuid, below `@solana/web3.js`) are listed and not fixed.
  - **`@solana/spl-token` is a dev dependency.** `bigint-buffer` came into the production install only through `@solana/spl-token` → `@solana/buffer-layout-utils`, and it has no patched release (the newest spl-token, 0.4.15, still depends on it). The server used four things of spl-token, all in `src/server/solanaRpc.js`: the Token program id, the account size (165 bytes), a wallet's associated token address, and the mint, owner and amount of an account (the amount through `toBigIntLE()`). They are now in `src/server/splTokenAccount.js`, which uses only `@solana/web3.js` and reads the amount with `Buffer.readBigUInt64LE`; `solanaRpc.js` makes the same checks in the same order with the same messages, and a wallet off the ed25519 curve is still refused before any request (its balance is unknown). spl-token and the 15 packages below it that nothing else needs (`bigint-buffer` among them) are no longer in a production install (`npm ci --omit=dev`, which the production smoke runs and checks; the image's `npm prune --omit=dev`). `npm ci` still installs them: the tests use the real package to write account data and addresses (`test/solanaRpc.test.js`, `test/e2e/token-gate.mjs`) and to check the new module against it (`test/splTokenAccount.test.js`: the address for 200 random wallets and random mints, owners off the curve, accounts written with random fields and amounts at the edges of a u64). As before, only the Token program's 165-byte accounts are read: not Token-2022, not accounts with extensions.
  - **Production install smoke.** The last step of CI (`scripts/prod-smoke.mjs`) copies the built tree to a temp folder, runs `npm ci --omit=dev` there, starts the server and expects `/health`, `/` and `/env.js` to answer 200 and a SIGTERM to end it with exit code 0. A SIGINT or SIGTERM to the script itself (Ctrl-C) stops the install or the server it started, removes the folder and exits with 130 or 143; a SIGKILL leaves both. It does not build or run the Docker image: not Alpine/musl, not the image's own install and prune, not its health check command; and no browser, WebSocket connection or existing world is involved.
  - **`no-eval`.** eslint reports `eval` as an error in `src/`, `scripts/` and `agent.mjs` (off for `test/**`, like `no-implied-eval` and `no-new-func`; the one place that names it there is an e2e probe app, `test/e2e/apps/sandbox-probe.js`, which reads `typeof eval`). No use is left in those three places. The rule does not cover the lint-ignored vendored folders or app scripts, which run in SES compartments.
- apps: `world.open(url)` asks the visitor before it opens a page outside the world's origin (P12: N10; the owner's decision). It used to send the tab to any `https` page, or open one in a new tab, with no click and no question: a script could take a visitor to a look-alike of the world, or to a wallet's send link.
  - **The question.** A dialog names the host on a line of its own and shows the address, both as the URL parser gives them (an international name in its `xn--` form, everything else that is not ASCII percent-encoded; an address longer than 300 characters is cut in what is shown, not in what is opened), and says whether it opens in a new tab or leaves the world. The page opens from the visitor's press on Open, which makes it a real click for the browser's pop-up rules; Cancel and Escape close the dialog. The dialog focuses nothing and a pointer press does not focus a button, so a key used in the world (Space, Enter) does not answer. Open is in practice a pointer or touch press: Tab does not move the focus in the world, only out of a text field such as the chat, so a visitor who can use only the keyboard can cancel (Escape) and cannot open.
  - **A second before Open works.** A press on Open counts only after the question has been on screen for a second without one; an earlier press starts the second again, and so does the tab coming back from the background. A visitor who is clicking fast where the button appears does not answer by those clicks.
  - **One at a time.** A call made while a question is on screen is dropped with a console warning: not queued, and not shown in place of the first.
  - **Not asked, not opened.** In an immersive (VR) session, and in a client without the interface. A question that waits when a session starts, or when the visitor hides the interface (Z), is cancelled, and nothing is asked while the interface is hidden: the link does nothing then, with a console warning. A page of the world's own origin (same scheme, host and port) opens as before, with no question.
  - **Also refused now.** An address with a user name or password in it (`https://this-world.example@other.example/`), and one whose host name is longer than 253 characters, a trailing dot not counted (DNS has no longer name, and thousands of characters pushed the buttons off the screen). Whatever the address, the dialog's text scrolls and its buttons stay in view.
  - **What this is not.** A check of the page. A script chooses the address and the moment, can ask again after a Cancel, and can draw its own text in the world around the dialog; the dialog is the world's own interface, above what scripts draw, and that is all it guarantees. Scripts are not told the answer.
  - **For app authors.** A link to another site (the NFT door's "Get it here", `docs/scripting/examples/nft-door.js`) now takes two presses: the one in the world, and Open. `docs/scripting/world/World.md` describes it.
- server: session tokens end. A new token lasts 180 days without being used, and every connect with a token older than a day gets a new one in the join snapshot (the client and the node agent store what the snapshot carries; the client now stores it before anything else in the snapshot is applied). A user who keeps coming back never notices. A user whose token ends unused joins as a new visitor: the name, avatar and rank stored with the old user are not theirs any more (wallets and tips are not tied to it). When that user had a rank the server logs it, so the operator can grant the rank again. Tokens made before this release have no end and stay valid until the secret is changed; they are renewed, with an end, the first time they are used.
- client: `world.evm.tip()` takes `onIntent({ request, confirms })`, called once the tip intent has arrived and before the wallet is asked anything, and `unconfirmedShown` and `onUnconfirmed({ request })`: when the intent says that the world is not confirming tips and `unconfirmedShown` is not `true`, the send waits for `onUnconfirmed` to resolve `true`, and anything else ends it as `declined` with nothing asked of the wallet; a `true` that comes 5 minutes or more (of the page's time) after the intent arrived ends it as `intent_expired`, with nothing asked of the wallet either. Its result carries `request` (`{ name, address, amount, token, at }`, the address in its short form) when the wallet was asked to send. `world.evm.getTipMarkers()` gives the notes of tip requests, newest first, `dismissTipMarker(id)` removes one, and the client event `evm:tipMarkers` carries the notes after a send that asked the wallet and after a Dismiss. The client result `bad_tx` is gone (see Fixed: `outcome_unknown`); the server's `bad_tx` for a malformed report is unchanged. `evmWalletStatus` and `evmTipIntent` each carry one more field (see Added); no packet was added and the protocol version is unchanged.
- docs: `World.md` says that the two players of a `tip` event may be one person (two wallets of one player, or two accounts; only the same wallet on both sides is refused), so a script must not take a tip as proof of a payment from someone else. It no longer advises telling repeats apart by `tipId`, which is the id of the tip request and not of the transaction: a transfer counted a second time comes with a new one. When that can happen is in the entry "one transfer is one tip" under Fixed; a script that grants something for a tip must tolerate it.
- server: `JWT_SECRET_PREVIOUS` (optional) makes a planned change of `JWT_SECRET` possible without every visitor becoming a new user and every hand-granted rank being lost: tokens signed with the previous secret are still accepted, never issued, and are renewed under the new one. Start-up refuses the public template value for it and says while it is set. It must not be used after a leak of the old secret.
- server: `/status` gives the number of connected users in `connectedUsers`, not a list. See Security.
- scripts, players: which avatar and emote URLs a player can make everyone else load (see Security). **Upgrade note:** a client script that calls `player.setSessionAvatar('https://…')` or `player.applyEffect({ emote: 'https://…' })` for the local player worked for every visitor; now a URL outside the world is shown to the other players only when that visitor is a builder. Do it from server code (`world.isServer`), or use an `asset://` file. A server script's `setSessionAvatar` is now applied by the server itself and sent to everyone as a change to the player (clients saw a `playerSessionAvatar` packet to the owner, then the owner's own change); an effect's `emote` that is not an emote URL is left out of the effect that is sent. A client that sends its player's session avatar is now told the session avatar the server has afterwards, whether its value was taken or not (a value it already has changes nothing there), so when a client's change and a server script's cross, every client, that one included, ends on the one the server took last. The client numbers these updates (`sessionAvatarRequest`: a whole number the server hands back in its answer to that client alone, and keeps, relays and snapshots nowhere) and applies only the answer to its latest one, so a player who chooses again before the answer to an earlier choice arrives does not see the earlier avatar again on the way. An update the server never gets (the inbound gate dropped it) is not answered, and that client keeps showing its own latest choice, as before.
- server: a stored avatar that is a URL outside the world shows as the world's default avatar while its user is not a builder: from the join, and from the moment a builder stops being one (an admin lowers the rank, `/admin` again, a token grant ends, the world's rank setting is lowered). The stored value is kept, and shows again from a later join as a builder. A builder who stops being one also loses a session avatar or emote outside the world that no server script chose; everyone, the player too, is told with the packet of any other change to the player. A stored value is read as the loaders read it, resolved: `//host/a.vrm`, `host/a.vrm` or an `https://` URL with white space around it is a URL outside the world too, and the same spellings of a file of the world's own store (`//world.example/assets/a.vrm` or `world.example/assets/a.vrm` with the assets at `https://world.example/assets`) are the user's own, with a query or a fragment or not. A page path (`/assets/a.vrm`) counts as outside: the browser resolves it against the page, and the server cannot know that the page is served where the assets are. The `avatar` parameter of a connection (the node agent's `AGENT_AVATAR`) is taken as a URL outside the world only for a builder.
- server: where no admin code is set (`locked`: nobody becomes admin by typing a code), an admin can no longer lower their own rank from the players list: there was no way back but an edit of the database. They are told why and nothing is changed. A guard against a slip of the hand, not a rule that an admin remains: an admin can still lower every other admin, and two can lower each other. With a code, or in an open world, nothing changes.
- docs: the README says what the builder rank is: `pinned`, `scene`, `locked` and `frozen` are conveniences for builders, changeable by any builder and not enforced by the server (enforcing them would stop nobody who holds the rank).
- client: the Custom Avatars setting says what it does: visitors may upload their own VRM, to this world.
- docker: the image's health check asks `/health`, not `/status`.
- server: `/admin <code>` answers a wrong code ("Wrong admin code.", to that player only) and slows guessing down (`src/server/adminCode.js`; see Security). After three wrong codes a user id and an address (an IPv4 address or an IPv6 /64) wait 10, 20, 40 and then 60 seconds between attempts; an address's /56 and /48 wait 10 seconds after 32. During a wait no code is looked at, the right one included, and each connection is told once per wait. Waits are measured on a monotonic clock, so a change of the system time does not move them. The table of recent failures holds 8,192 keys; when room is short, user ids give way before addresses, and an attempt from an address that finds no room is not looked at either (it is answered as a wait, and that connection waits 10 seconds). A connection is closed at its 21st attempt (kick code `admin_attempts`). The client and the server decide what is the admin command in one place (`src/core/extras/chatCommand.js`): a first word that is `admin` in any letter case once trimmed, so `/Admin`, `/ admin` and the like are the admin command on both sides, and none of them reaches scripts as a `command` event, on the client or on the server.
- moderation: the kicked player's overlay says "You were removed by a moderator." (and "Try again in a few minutes." when they come back too early); `agent.mjs` stops on the `kicked` code as it does on `moderation`. Kicks are logged with both user ids.
- server: a world's admin mode is decided once from the environment: a non-empty `ADMIN_CODE`; open to all (no code outside production, or `ADMIN_OPEN=true`); or, **in production with no code and no `ADMIN_OPEN=true`, locked**: nobody is an admin by default and `/admin` does nothing, where every visitor used to be an admin. Stored ranks apply in every mode. The server starts in all three and says at start-up which it is (never the code or its length). It also warns about a code under 16 characters (the brake still allows thousands of guesses a day from each network, so only a long random code holds), and about one the chat cannot carry: one with a space in it or whitespace at either end, or one longer than the 493 characters a chat line leaves after `/admin `. `/status.protected` is false only for an open world (it was true for an empty `ADMIN_CODE`).
- server: start-up logs where client addresses are read from (the configured header or the connection). The per-address limits for connections, uploads and wrong admin codes all use it; behind a proxy that is not named in `UPLOAD_CLIENT_IP_HEADER` every client has the proxy's address.
- scripts: the numbers and texts of a node that become a size are kept inside a range (`src/core/extras/boundedValue.js`). A value outside its range becomes the nearest bound, `NaN` becomes the property's default, a text over its limit is cut; nothing new throws, and reading the property back gives what was kept. These are not a defence against a builder who wants to stall visitors (a script can loop); they keep a wrong value (a bug, a `NaN`, `1e9`, a pasted book) from costing every visitor the tab.
  - **UI.** `width` and `height` 0 to 16,384, `res` 0 to 16, `size` 0.000001 to 1,000,000. The canvas has at most 4,096 pixels on a side and 8,388,608 in all; a larger UI keeps its size, layout and pointer coordinates and is drawn at a lower resolution. (A canvas over the browser's own limit cannot be drawn on, and each pixel costs memory twice: in the canvas and in its texture.)
  - **Text.** `uitext.value` 10,000 characters and `fontSize` 0 to 1,024; a nametag's and an action's `label` 256 characters.
  - **Particles.** `max` a whole number up to 20,000, `rate` up to 100,000, at most 64 `bursts` with a `count` up to 20,000, `duration` at least 0.01 s, `timescale` 0 to 100; spritesheet rows and columns 1 to 256 and its frame rate 0 to 1,000 (one that is not a number, or is 0 or less, shows the first frame); a start value (`life`, `speed`, `size`, `rotate`, `color`, `alpha`, `emissive`) 256 characters and an over-life curve 4,096 (64 points of the longest numbers JavaScript prints fit), of which the worker reads up to 64 points and two values a point. In the worker, one step starts at most as many particles as the pool holds over its rate, distance and bursts together, and looks for a free particle from where it found the last one: a step costs the pool's length once (it was the pool's length per particle started, and a rate without end with particles of no life never returned).
  - **Audio, video.** `volume` 0 to 100, and the spatial numbers inside what Web Audio accepts (`refDistance` and `rolloffFactor` not negative, `maxDistance` above zero, cone angles 0 to 360, `coneOuterGain` 0 to 1): a value outside threw in the middle of `play()`, or in the setter of a node that was playing. An audio node's `currentTime` and a video's `time` (`currentTime`) are finite and not negative (`NaN` is 0). `audio.setPlaybackRate(rate)` reads the rate by the engine's number rule and keeps it between -1,000 and 1,000 (`NaN` is 1): a rate that is not finite as a float (`NaN`, `Infinity`, `1e100`) threw from the ramp of a playing sound; on a sound that is not playing, and on the server, it does nothing (the server threw).
  - **Sky, image, video.** The sky's `rotationY`, `sunIntensity`, `fogNear` and `fogFar` are finite (`NaN` counts as not set; one `NaN` there blacked out the scene). An image's or video's `width` and `height` are finite and not negative (`NaN` counts as not set), a video's `aspect` 0.001 to 1,000.
- scripts: `node.cursor` takes CSS cursor keywords only (`pointer`, `grab`, `crosshair`, ...); see Security. Of an image cursor with its fallback (`url(hand.png), pointer`) the closing keyword is used and read back; a value without a keyword reads back as `null` and the default cursor shows. One warning is logged; nothing throws.
- models: a collider in a model file gets a layer a script could set. A `layer` custom property that is not `environment`, `prop` or `tool` (the engine's own `player` layer included, which a script's handle already refused) gets the default (`environment`); the model still loads. This is one rule for both ways in, not a new protection: the layer carries no authority.
- client: a player whose avatar cannot be loaded (a missing file, one the loader's limits refuse, a file that is not a usable avatar) appears with the built-in avatar. Such a player was a name over nothing, for everyone, for as long as they stayed. A player who already has an avatar showing keeps it when a new one fails, also when the new one cannot be made, placed or mounted, or fails once it is placed (its heights cannot be read, the name or the bubble cannot be moved): the new avatar is mounted and set up before the old one goes, and one that fails on the way is taken out again, leaving the player as it was; its failed mount is reported with the load, not as an unhandled rejection. A load turned away only because the loader was busy is asked for once more after 15 seconds, whichever avatar shows meanwhile; a later choice (even of the same avatar) has its own load, and the earlier retry does nothing.
- client: `window.world`, `window.THREE`, `window.app`, `window.prims` and `window.preview` are set only on a page served from localhost, or when `PUBLIC_DEBUG=true` (`.env.example`). This is not a protection: code that runs in the page can reach the world without these names. It keeps a stable name for console snippets off a deployed site. Debug `console.log` calls that printed AI prompts, a script's `world.open()` target, undo and avatar preview details are removed.
- scripts (behavior changes from the script → host boundary work, see Security):
  - `world.chat(msg)`: only `body` and `from` are read; `fromId`, `id`, `kind` and any other field are ignored. The message is an app line (`kind: 'app'`, the app's name in `app`), never a player's line, so it no longer pops a player's bubble and the `chat` event it fires has no `fromId`. A client script's broadcast goes to the server as a new `appChat` packet and the server relays it as that app's line (it used to arrive as the visitor's own chat).
  - `app.fetch()` rejects with a built-in error (as the standard `fetch` does) when the request fails; it used to log and resolve `undefined`. A request still open when its app is rebuilt, crashes or is removed is aborted and never settles, without a log line.
  - `props` (and `app.props`/`app.config`) is the instance's own copy: changes stay in that instance until its next build, and `configure`'s `initial` values no longer reach the shared blueprint or the join snapshot. A blueprint without props gives the script `{}` (it was `null`). Exported `.hyp` files still carry each field's `initial` for keys the app does not set, as before; duplicating an app copies only the props the builder set (the copy's script applies its own initial values).
  - `app.configure()`: a field whose `key` is not a string of 1-64 characters, or is `__proto__`/`constructor`/`prototype`, is dropped with one console warning per app (a `section` or `button` may still have no key); properties the sidebar does not use are dropped; at most 256 fields. `when` rules see a field's `initial` value when the prop is unset.
  - `control.setActions()`: at most 64 actions, labels cut to 128 characters; the objects passed in are not changed (no `id` is added).
  - Nodes: adding a node to itself or to its own descendant throws; more than 20,000 live nodes or 256 levels in one app crashes it. A boxed `String` is no longer accepted as a nametag label (throws as other non-strings) or an action label (shows the default).
  - `setTimeout` throws past 1,024 pending timers; calls from a removed app's script (after its `destroy` event) throw.
  - `world.raycast()` and the player/bone/world-matrix getters listed under Security return a new object each call.
  - The engine's math objects a handle gives out by reference (a node's `position`, `quaternion`, `rotation`, `scale` and `matrixWorld`; a joint's offsets, rotations and `axis`; a UI's `offset`; a control's camera, poses and sticks; a skinned-mesh bone's transform) are sealed: their values change as before, but adding, redefining or deleting a property throws a `TypeError`, a matrix's `elements` cannot be replaced, a component keeps a number (a string, a boolean or `null` given to one is converted, as `node.position.x = '2'` and `.fromArray(text.split(','))` expect; `undefined`, a Symbol, a BigInt and any object, a boxed `new Number(2)` included, are `NaN`, an object's `valueOf` never called; an Euler order that is not a string leaves the order), and `_onChangeCallback` cannot be replaced (a script could replace it, and the engine then ran the script's function on every change, inside a physics step or a commit). A node's own `position`, `quaternion`, `rotation` and `scale` are the engine's objects; a value written straight into one of their fields (`_x`) is read by the engine by the same rule (an Euler `_order` that is not a string as `'YXZ'`). The rest (`matrixWorld` and its `elements`, joint, UI, control, bone and particles vectors) are views of the engine's objects that behave like them and keep numbers in their fields by the same rule. A control's entries (`camera`, buttons, poses, sticks, `scrollDelta`, `pointer`, `screen`) are sealed: their fields can be written as before (`camera.write`, `camera.zoom`, a button's `capture` and `onPress`), but a field cannot become an accessor, nothing can be added or deleted, and the engine's parts (the camera's and a pose's vectors, a stick's `value`, the pointer's and screen's getters) cannot be replaced. A rigidbody's `getPosition()`, `getQuaternion()` and velocity getters called without a target return a new object each time (they returned one kept per body). A particles node's `force`, `velocityLinear` and `velocityOrbital` are engine copies of the vectors set (reading one back gives the copy, not the script's object).
  - A call whose own arguments end its app's run (a getter, `toString` or `valueOf` that removes or rebuilds the app) makes nothing and throws `[app] this app has stopped`. Names and types are converted once, before anything is made: an event name (`app.on`, `world.on`), a node type (`app.create`), a `world.load()` url, a skinned mesh's clip name (and its `fade` and `speed`, as numbers). `setVoiceLevel()` takes a string only; any other value is reported and changes nothing (it used to reach the voice system, which refused it). `world.getPlayers()` throws the stopped error from the player stage on also when nobody is in the world.
  - Node setters keep plain values: one that takes a number, a string or a boolean throws for a boxed primitive (`new Number(1)`) or a Proxy that poses as one (lodash's checks accepted both); `node.active`, a joint's limits and `collide`, and `lod.insert(node, maxDistance)` throw for an object. A UI's `padding`, `margin` and `scaler` and a particles node's `shape`, `bursts` and `spritesheet` read back as the node's frozen copy (changing it throws a `TypeError`), and an entry of a particles `shape` or `spritesheet` or of a UI `scaler` must be a primitive (a `scaler` used to be kept as the script's own array, read every frame). An `applyEffect` field that is an object or a function (`emote`, `snare`, `turn`, `duration`, ...) refuses the effect, as an invalid value does. `app.off` and `world.off` convert the event name as `on` does.
  - A node handle's setters act on the handle they are called on or on an object that inherits from it (`Object.create(node)`); a setter copied to another object or called without a receiver throws `[node] not a node`, and one called on the handle of a node without that property throws `[node] a group node has no castShadow to set` (it threw a raw `TypeError` from `Function.prototype.apply`). Methods keep their `name` and `length`. A handle follows its node: when another app's live run places the node, every handle of it (also one the first app kept) works for that run.
  - Handles belong to the app run that got them: after a rebuild, crash rebuild or removal, the old run's `world`, `app` and player objects throw `[app] this app has stopped`, its control answers but changes nothing (`setActions`, `hideReticle`), its `fetch` and body reads never settle, and its nodes cannot be mounted again with `active = true`. Every acting player method (`damage`, `heal`, `applyEffect`, `cancelEffect`, `setSessionAvatar`, `teleport`, `push`, `screenshare`, `setVoiceLevel`) now also refuses a crashed app, as other calls do (an effect handle's `cancel` refuses once the run has ended); the app's own `destroy` handler works as before. Player objects no longer have `$cleanup`.
  - A released control's action hints leave the HUD and it can no longer hide the reticle.
  - `world.overlapSphere()` returns new plain `{ tag, playerId }` records; raycast and overlap report a `tag` only when it is a string, and a rigidbody `tag` must be one (a boxed `String` throws). `world.get()` returns a copy of the stored value.
  - `control.pointer.coords/position/delta` are new vectors on each read; an app's `onTouch`/`onTouchEnd` receive a copy of the touch; a camera with `write` moves only to finite values.
  - The engine's `enter`, `leave`, `health` and `command` world events are frozen copies.
  - Particles `shape` and `bursts`, UI `margin`/`padding` and Sky `sunDirection` are the node's own copies (reads of `sunDirection` return copies); a non-finite `sunDirection` throws.
  - Action `distance`/`duration` and Nametag `health` must be finite primitive numbers (a boxed `Number`, `NaN` or `Infinity` throws); a node `cursor` must be a string of at most 128 characters or `null`.
  - Moving a mounted node (also one in world space) under another node remounts it there; a node never placed in the world is not mounted by `active = true`; `world.add` of a node mounted for another app moves it.
  - `setTimeout` with a delay that is not a number or a numeric string uses `0` (its `valueOf` is not called).
  - A client script's `world.chat(msg, true)` reaches the others as an app line through the new `appChat` packet (see Security).
  - Persisted settings and the join snapshot go through the settings rules; an invalid stored value is replaced by that key's fallback (logged once).
  - `material.color` (mesh materials) returns a new `Color` with the material's values; changing it changes nothing (assign a CSS colour string, as documented). Material `textureX`/`textureY`/`emissiveIntensity` take finite primitive numbers and `fog` a boolean; a video's `material.textureX`/`textureY` take finite numbers.
  - `world.getPlayer()` handles are per app run and are no longer replaced when a script changes `destroyed` on one; a player looked up from a cleanup callback (an effect's `onEnd`) while the app is being torn down throws `[app] this app has stopped`.
  - `fetch` and `setTimeout` are bound to the run that received them: kept and called after a rebuild, `setTimeout` throws `[app] this app has stopped` and `fetch` starts nothing and never settles.
  - A world-space node another app takes over (its `world.add`, or moving it under its own node) leaves the first app's world-space list: the first app's `world.remove` and teardown no longer touch it.
  - An app's `onTouch`/`onTouchEnd` receive one frozen touch object per touch (the same at start and end) whose `position`, `prevPosition` and `delta` follow the touch as new vectors on each read. `control.pointer.lock()`/`unlock()` do nothing once the control is released. XR ray poses no longer carry the engine's smoothing state (`lPosition`/`lQuaternion`).
  - Action nodes' default `onStart`/`onTrigger`/`onCancel` are frozen functions; the engine calls script callbacks with `Reflect.apply`, so a `call`/`apply` property on a callback is not used.
  - `mesh.material` and `video.material` are frozen objects bound to the run of the app the node is mounted for: the same object within the run (also for meshes of the run that share a linked material), another object for another app. Setting a property on one kept after that app stopped throws `[app] this app has stopped`; reading still works, and the app's own `destroy` handler can still set it.
  - Another app's root (what its `app` stands for) cannot be moved: `world.add`, `world.attach` and `node.add` throw `[…] cannot add another app's root node`. An app moving its own root works as before, except under a node that is not placed anywhere yet.
  - While an app is torn down, an effect's `onEnd` (or any other cleanup callback) can end things through the app's player handles (`cancelEffect`, `setVoiceLevel(null)`) but `applyEffect` and `setVoiceLevel(level)` throw `[app] this app has stopped`.
  - A pointer lock taken through a control ends when that control is released (or its app stops or is rebuilt); a lock another control took is left alone, calling `app.control()` again hands the lock to the new control, and the control's own `onRelease` can still call `pointer.unlock()`. A touch object kept after its touch ended or its control was released answers with the touch's last values.
  - `app.control()` throws `[app] this app has stopped` once the app's teardown has started (in a `destroy` handler, an `onRelease` or an effect's `onEnd` run by the teardown); a control of an app run that has ended does nothing. During `onRelease`, `pointer.unlock()` works only for a lock the control still holds. `video.material` is `undefined` until the video is mounted (and once it is unmounted), as `mesh.material` is. Pointer callbacks (`onPointerEnter`/`Leave`/`Down`/`Up`) of an app run that has ended are not called. While an app is removed, its own cleanup callbacks can still end its effect and voice level (`cancelEffect`, `setVoiceLevel(null)`).
  - App teardown (see App.md, Teardown) distinguishes what a run owns from effects on shared state. What it owns (nodes and what they hold, controls, player effects and voice levels, listeners, timers, requests, object URLs) is released at teardown and cannot be made again by the run once its stage has begun: `world.getPlayer()`/`getPlayers()` and starting effects or voice levels from the player stage on; `world.add`/`world.attach` and every method and setter of a node of the run from the node stage on, and for good once the run has ended (`[app] this app has stopped`; getters keep reading); listeners, `fetch`, object URLs and `setTimeout` from their own stages. Effects on shared or persistent state (chat, sends, storage, teleport/push/damage/heal, session avatar, screenshare, wallet queries, `world.open`) are allowed while the run is alive, including in the callbacks a removal's teardown runs (they used to be refused after the `destroy` event), and refused once it has ended. A node added under a node that is unmounting is not mounted; an unmounted sound, skinned mesh or UI image does not play, animate or load. `world.getPlayer(id)` returns `null` for a player that is leaving and `world.getPlayers()` leaves it out; a handle whose player has left starts no effect or voice level (`[player] this player has left`). `URL.createObjectURL` URLs belong to the run whose `fetch` made the blob (at most 256 live, all revoked when the run ends); `URL.revokeObjectURL` is new and revokes only those.
- client: a model, avatar or emote may only use what is inside its file. A `.gltf` or `.glb` that names an external buffer (a `.bin` by URL or relative path) fails its load; one that names an external image loads without that texture. Embedded buffers and images, and `data:` URIs, load as before. Until now a file's absolute dependency URLs (`https://…`) were fetched, for an uploaded `.glb` or `.vrm` as for a remote file, outside the loader's size limits and deadlines; only relative ones never resolved (files are parsed without a base path). A file that relied on absolute URLs now fails (buffers) or loads without those textures (images). The server cannot load external files either.
- client: an image a model, avatar or emote decodes must be a PNG, JPEG or WebP, by its bytes whatever `mimeType` it declares; a file with any other image it decodes (GIF, BMP) fails its load with a reason. An image used only through `KHR_texture_basisu` is never decoded (this client has no KTX2 decoder and uses the fallback image). AVIF textures inside models are not decoded either, in any browser: the parser takes `EXT_texture_avif` as unsupported and uses the fallback image (an AVIF image cannot be sized before its decode; a file that requires the extension fails its load with a reason). Stand-alone AVIF images still go to the browser as before. An image named by URL is not fetched, as before. The bundled models and avatar decode PNG and WebP only. Stand-alone images keep every format the browser decodes.
- client: an asset in the world's own store is loaded and kept once whatever its query or hash: `asset://x.glb?s=2` and `asset://x.glb` are one download and one result, and `loader.get`/`has` answer for either. Playback options in an emote's query (`?s=`, `?l=`, `?g=`) still apply, since the avatar reads them from the URL it was given. A URL from another host keeps its query, which can name a different resource.
- server: remote models, emotes and scripts named by a blueprint are fetched through one outbound request policy (`src/core/extras/outboundFetch.js`). App scripts' `fetch` goes through it too, on the server and the node agent (in a browser it is the browser's fetch, as before): see **Scripts** below. Avatars are stubs on the server and are not fetched.
  - **Where it may connect.** `http` and `https` only, no credentials in the URL, and only public addresses. IPv4: loopback, private, link-local (cloud metadata), carrier-NAT and the other special ranges are refused. IPv6: an address is refused when it is not global unicast (`2000::/3`), so loopback, link-local, unique-local, multicast, NAT64 and the IPv4-compatible and IPv4-translated forms are refused, or when it is in a special-purpose range inside it (the `2001::/23` protocol assignments such as Teredo and ORCHID, documentation, 6to4) or in the returned 6bone space `3ffe::/16`. An IPv4-mapped address follows the IPv4 rules. No allocation table is kept: an unallocated block inside `2000::/3` is admitted (it has no route on the public internet), so new address space works once it is allocated. The host is resolved once through DNS (A and AAAA; the hosts file is not consulted), every address must be public, and the connection is made to a checked address, so a second DNS answer cannot change the destination. The first IPv4 answer is used, or the first IPv6 one; if connecting to it fails (refused or unreachable) or has not connected within a third of the time left, but at least 250 ms, one more checked answer is tried, of the other family when there is one. That attempt is still within the one deadline: a deadline shorter than the first attempt's 250 ms ends the request before it. The connected address is checked again before anything is sent, and TLS is verified for the URL's name. Redirects (at most 3) pass the same checks, and the body of a redirect that is followed is not read.
  - **Limits.** One deadline covers the whole request: resolving, connecting, redirects and the body. A remote load may take 30 s and 32 MiB of decoded body. At most 16 requests run at once and more are refused rather than queued; a request keeps its slot until its DNS queries have ended. The slots cover the exchanges only, not what a caller does with a response afterwards (the loader's parsing has its own bound, below). Responses being received share a 64 MiB budget, which covers a request until its response is handed over (the body is held once, as received); what the caller then reads or keeps is the caller's memory. The handed-over body still follows the caller's `signal`, as fetch's does: an abort before it is read to the end (an empty body included) makes reading it reject with the signal's reason, an `AbortError` for a plain `abort()`. A response without a body (HEAD, 204, 205, 304) has nothing to abort: reading it after an abort gives `''`, as native fetch does on Node 24 (on Node 22 native fetch rejects). Bodies are decoded from gzip and deflate only, and a response without a body (HEAD, 204, 205, 304) is not decoded.
  - **Requests.** The options are fetch's: `method` (GET, HEAD, POST, PUT, PATCH, DELETE, OPTIONS), `headers` (repeated names are combined as fetch combines them, Cookie with `; `; every header sent, the ones the policy adds included (Host, Accept-Encoding, `Connection: close`, Content-Length, which is also `0` for a POST, PUT or PATCH without a body, and an inferred Content-Type), is set explicitly, so Node adds none, and is validated and counted before anything is resolved or sent: at most 64 and 16 KiB of names and values in all, so a GET, HEAD, DELETE or OPTIONS without a body leaves 61 to the caller and a POST of a string 59; a Blob's type must be a string), `body` (a string, an ArrayBuffer, a typed array or DataView, or a Blob, at most 1 MiB, with the Content-Type fetch infers), `signal` and `redirect` (`follow`, `manual` returns the redirect, `error` rejects; an empty `Location` is the same URL again). A request that a redirect turns into a GET loses its body and fetch's request-body headers (Content-Encoding, Content-Language, Content-Location, Content-Type). `mode`, `credentials` and `cache` mean nothing on the server and are ignored; any other option, and a GET or HEAD with a body, is refused.
  - **Trusted origins.** The world's own assets URL is exempt from the address check (it is local in development and may be a private object store). `OUTBOUND_TRUSTED_ORIGINS` lists further origins, comma separated. Upgrade note: a world whose blueprints load from a host on a private network must list that origin.
  - **Scripts.** A script's `fetch()` on the server used the global fetch, so a script could request the server's own loopback, its private network and cloud metadata addresses, with responses of any size. It is now made by the same policy (an instance of its own, so scripts' requests and the server's remote loads cannot take each other's slots or memory), with the app as the caller: public addresses only (and the trusted origins), a response of at most 8 MiB of decoded body, a request body of at most 1 MiB, 15 s for the whole request, at most 3 redirects, and at most 4 requests in flight per app (16 for all apps together); a request beyond those is refused, not queued, and its promise rejects with a `TypeError` whose message says why (`outbound request refused: …`, `outbound request failed: …`). The response resolves once its whole body has arrived (it used to resolve at the headers), so an endpoint that keeps its response open fails at the 15 s. The headers the policy sets or drops (Host, Connection, Content-Length, Accept-Encoding, Transfer-Encoding, Proxy-Authorization and the other hop-by-hop ones) are no longer the script's to send, and a decompressed response has no `content-encoding` or `content-length` header. A request body is sized and sent by its own bytes: an `ArrayBuffer` or view is copied by the host without running the constructor it names (`Symbol.species`) or reading `byteLength`, `byteOffset` or `buffer` properties a script put on it (a subclass with an own `byteLength` of 1 used to pass the request-size check with a megabyte of bytes). A `data:` URL and an object URL made for a script are still read locally, by the platform's fetch (no other `blob:` URL of the process is); the limits above are those of `http`/`https` requests and do not apply to these reads, which hand over bytes the script already holds. They are read to the end without the run's abort signal: on Node 24, aborting the signal of such a response while its body was unread (an app removed or rebuilt after `fetch('data:…')`) threw from the platform's abort listener and ended the server process; Node 22, which the Docker image pins, is not affected. Upgrade notes: a server-side script that calls a service on a private address needs that origin in `OUTBOUND_TRUSTED_ORIGINS`; one that starts more than 4 requests at once must wait for some to finish; a response compressed with anything but gzip or deflate is refused (the request asks for those only). See docs/scripting/Utils.md, Fetch.
  - **Models.** On the server a model or emote may only use what is inside its file (`data:` and `blob:` URIs): nothing outside the file is ever requested. An external buffer fails the load; an external image is not fetched, and the model loads without it (the server draws nothing).
  - **Cache.** The server loader keeps at most 512 finished loads of every kind and location (the world's store and remote URLs alike), the least recently used leaving first. A load in progress is never dropped, so while more than 512 are in progress the cache holds them all. What loads in progress hold is bounded per kind: models and emotes, local and remote alike, are read and parsed in 4 turns (a turn covers the read and the parse, and the others wait in order without having read anything). A deadline frees a turn whose read (30 s) or parse (60 s) does not settle: that load fails and the turn goes to the next. A read given up is cancelled (its outbound request is aborted, a local read stops); a parse cannot be, so a parse whose turn expired may still be running, and holding its memory, until it settles, outside the 4 turns. A deadline cannot interrupt a parse that blocks the event loop, and a phase that completes before its timer has run is accepted however long it took, so a large but valid model still loads on a slow host. A destroyed loader fails the loads waiting and those in a turn, and starts nothing for them, a remote script takes one of the 16 outbound slots while it downloads, and a local script read has no bound of its own. A load that finishes after it was replaced or after the loader was destroyed writes nothing. One resource has one entry whatever its spelling (surrounding spaces, a fragment, a default port); an `asset://` name and the assets URL it maps to share one where assets are read over HTTP, while with a local assets directory the file and the URL are separate resources and keep separate entries. A query names another resource. Failed loads are not kept, so a URL that starts working is loaded on the next request. Every avatar shares one entry.
- scripts: `world.set` has limits, and `world.get` returns a copy. A key is a string of 1 to 128 characters, a value at most 64 KiB as JSON, at most 1,024 keys and 4 MiB in total (`STORAGE_MAX_KEYS`, `STORAGE_MAX_VALUE_BYTES`, `STORAGE_MAX_BYTES`). A `set` past a limit throws and leaves the store as it was; it used to grow without bound, and each write rewrote the whole file.
