- server: the player limit was asked once, when a connection arrived, and the player was counted only when registered, after waits on the database and the voice token: any number of visitors arriving together all found room. Joins being set up are counted now, by place: a user has one place, shared by their registered connection and every join of theirs under way (a join whose session token names them), and a join of no known user (no token, or one whose user row is gone) has one of its own. A join takes its place, or is refused, once its token is read and before any wait, and gives it back when it is registered, refused or fails, or when its connection closes, also in the middle of a wait that never ends (before, a closed join kept its place and its user id until its wait ended, and that user's next connection was refused as a duplicate meanwhile). A player who connects again (a reload) shares their place, also while their old connection is closed, so nobody else takes it meanwhile. A limit lowered while joins are being set up does not turn away those that hold a place.
- server: an app whose builder left in the middle of moving it stayed in the move for everyone, and one whose builder left in the middle of an upload showed "loading" for good; the changes made while it was held were not saved (an app that is held is not written: a moved app kept its last saved row, an upload in progress had none). When a connection ends, a move it held is let go where the app is (said to everyone, and saved), and an upload it never reported finished is taken as not finished: the app is removed, as the uploader's own client does when an upload fails. A connection that another one of the same user has replaced releases nothing.
- client: a builder who had an app selected when it was removed (by another builder, or by the server when its uploader left) placed it again when letting go: a change was sent for an app that is gone, and its model was mounted again, with the app no longer in the world. The check was on a field no app has (`dead`). An app removed while selected is let go without either, and a removed app is not built again.
- client: the local player's switch to first person no longer throws when no avatar has loaded, and an avatar that arrives while the view is in first person is hidden from it (it showed inside the camera until the view changed).
- client, server: a model's `data:` URI can no longer block the thread that parses it. The vendored GLTFLoader handed every buffer and image URI to three.js's `LoaderUtils.resolveURL`, whose `/^data:.*,.*$/i` backtracks over every comma before a line break, quadratically: a 98 KB file (`data:application/octet-stream,`, 100,000 commas, a line break and one character) blocked the main thread for 5 s, a 1 MB one for minutes, and the parse's time limit cannot interrupt it. The server parses models with the same parser, without the check before it. The parser now uses a `data:` or `blob:` URI as it is (what `resolveURL` returns for one), found by a five-character prefix test; on the client the check also refuses a URI with a line break (see Security).
- client: a load that failed is tried again the next time the asset is asked for. The failure used to be kept for the session, so an asset whose upload finished a moment later could not be shown without a reload. An image that cannot be decoded now fails its load instead of leaving it pending for ever.
- client: a sky whose background or HDR cannot be loaded still applies its sun, fog and rotation; the asset that failed is treated as not set: no background (the previous sky's texture is let go of) and no environment map (the previous sky's HDR used to stay). The failure used to stop the whole update, as an unhandled rejection. It is reported once per URL (the last 64 failing URLs are remembered). Background and HDR now load side by side.
- client: a model whose primitives share an optional Draco extension but not their fallback data shows each primitive's own geometry; all of them showed the first one's (the parser's geometry cache keyed them by the Draco data, which this client does not decode).
- client: an image inside a model, avatar or emote whose decode fails releases its blob URL and the bytes it holds; only a decode that succeeded did. A decode still running when the file is refused releases it when it ends.
- client: a shift-click download from a file field or the app model button works for a file the loader no longer keeps (one from another host, past the loader's bound): it is downloaded again. The file field used to report "could not load file" and the model button did nothing.
- client: the start-up watchdog in the page could leave a black screen with no message. When its timer fired before the browser had the document's `<body>` (the head had arrived and the rest was slow or stalled), showing the message threw, and no later message could be shown. It now looks again until the body is there (without waiting for `DOMContentLoaded`, which a slow script in the body holds back). The e2e step covers a document whose parser is held in the head past the slow mark, and a wait for a message that does not come now reports what the page looked like instead of only timing out.
- nodes: an in-place edit of a UI node's `offset` (`ui.offset.x = 5`) now rebuilds the UI. The node used to overwrite the vector's `_onChange` method instead of registering a callback. A cloned UI node gets its own offset vector instead of sharing the source's.
- server: stopping the server saves the world first. SIGINT and SIGTERM used to close the listener and exit, so every stop (a deploy, an auto-stopped machine) lost up to `SAVE_INTERVAL` seconds of blueprint and app edits and the pending script-storage write.
  - **The stop.** The first signal stops the world (ticks and app timers), stops accepting connections and runs the final save under a 3.5 s deadline (inside Fly's default 5 s), then exits 0, or 1 with a list of what was not saved. A second signal exits at once.
  - **The deadline holds.** Database rows and queued config writes are written one at a time with a turn of the event loop between them and the clock checked before each, so a slow disk (better-sqlite3 writes synchronously) cannot run the save past its deadline or keep a second signal from being handled. Rows are not grouped in transactions: on Postgres one failing statement would fail the rest of its transaction. On Postgres the queries are asynchronous anyway.
  - **During start-up.** The handlers are installed before anything is loaded. A signal before the world exists exits 0 (there is nothing to save, and the log says so); from the moment the world is created, before its scripts start and before the server listens, a signal runs the final save.
  - **Listening.** The server listens only once the network has loaded the world, the user ids and the join-state accounting and has measured the join snapshot's fixed part (start-up used not to wait for that), and it refuses a connection or packet that comes earlier.
- server: `storage.json` (script storage) and uploaded assets are written to a temporary file, flushed, and renamed into place, so a stop or a full disk mid-write no longer leaves a cut-off file under the final name. The directory is flushed after the rename too; only where the filesystem cannot do that (`EINVAL`, `ENOTSUP`, `EOPNOTSUPP`, `EPERM`, `EISDIR`) is that step skipped, and any other failure counts as a failed write, which is retried. Storage writes no longer overlap.
  - **Unreadable storage.** A `storage.json` whose content is not a JSON object is moved to `storage.json.corrupt-<time>` and reported; it used to be replaced by an empty store without a word. A file that cannot be read at all (`EIO`, `EACCES`, `EMFILE`, …) is read again for about a second; if that still fails, it is left exactly as it is and the store is off for that run (`world.set` throws, `world.get` returns `undefined`), with one log line.
  - **Assets.** An asset whose stored size differs from an upload of the same content hash (a cut-off write from before) is replaced; unfinished uploads are removed at start-up and are never served. An upload whose directory flush failed (the file is complete but not known to be durable) is flushed again by the next upload of the same content before it is answered "already stored". The upload check (`/api/upload-check`) does the same: it waits for a write of that name in flight, flushes a name whose flush failed before it says the file exists, and says it does not when that fails again, so the client uploads and the upload tries once more.
- server: with `ASSETS=s3` the clean-up deleted nothing and logged success (`delete` was given a Set and read `.length`). The S3 content types now cover every uploadable extension. A key S3 answers with an error (`AccessDenied`, …) is reported as not deleted instead of being counted.
- core: `World.resolveURL` put `ASSETS_BASE_URL` into an `asset://` URL as a replacement string, so `$&`, `$'`, `` $` `` or `$$` in it changed the URL every loader requested (`https://host/$&/assets` made `asset://x.js` a request for `https://host/asset://assets/x.js`). The base is now put in literally.
- server: the clean-up (`CLEAN`) has a stated contract, keeps what the world names by its file name whatever URL it is written in, deletes nothing when it cannot read what it must, and, with `ASSETS=local`, moves what it removes to a trash instead of unlinking it.
  - **The contract.** It keeps every store file whose name appears as a literal in the persisted world data or in the source of a store script reachable from that data, and deletes nothing when it cannot read something it must read. The code it follows is exactly: the store file a blueprint's script names, whatever its extension, and any `.js` store file named as a literal in that data or in code it follows. It cannot see, so such a file is removed: a name computed at run time (`'abc' + 'def.glb'`, a template, a hash) or written with JavaScript escapes (`'\x61bc.glb'`); code built at run time, that is code a script fetches or assembles and evaluates itself (`fetch` and `Function`), wherever it is hosted, the store included, and whatever that code names (no engine API runs another script: `world.load` takes models and avatars only, and an app's script is its blueprint's); references inside binary assets (a model's external image or buffer URI, which the loaders do not follow: models, avatars and emotes use their embedded data). Worlds that rely on any of these must run with `CLEAN=false`.
  - **What keeps a file.** Its name, found anywhere in apps, the blueprints that stay and collection blueprints (both as stored and as hydration loads them, with model and script cut at 2,048 characters), settings, user avatars, script storage (parsed, so JSON escapes are decoded; a copy set aside as unreadable is read as text), and the source of the code it follows, to a fixed point (a `.js` name the store listing leaves out, which holds content-hash names only, is asked of the store, at most 4,096 of them, and names nothing when there is no such file; one that is there but cannot be read stops the clean-up). The text is cut at every character a store name cannot hold, and a file is kept when a piece equals its name in any case, also percent-decoded (a run of up to 4,096 characters, at most 8 times), without dots, `_` or `-` at its ends, and with tabs, CR and LF inside it dropped (as the URL parser drops them), in one loop with the decoding (each pass drops them, then decodes once), so escapes and dropped controls in any order and position are undone, and what decoding brings in (`%09`, `%0D`, `%0A`) is dropped too. No URL is resolved for this, so a host, port, path prefix, `..`, query or CDN spelling cannot hide a name; ignoring case keeps what a case-insensitive file system loads from `asset://<NAME>`. A file named like a common word is kept whenever that word appears.
  - **When nothing is removed** (logged once; in these configurations cleaning never runs and the store only grows): a database row or `storage.json` does not parse as JSON, or a row has the wrong shape; `storage.json`, a set-aside copy or a script it must read cannot be read, including a store script that is missing, in every configuration (a CDN or cache in front of the store may still serve it), one that cannot be opened (the log names the blueprint), a file larger than the larger of `PUBLIC_MAX_UPLOAD_SIZE` and `STORAGE_MAX_BYTES` (read before, whole, up to Node's own limits), and an S3 script object stored with a content encoding other than gzip or deflate, with more than two codings or expanding past that size (the clean-up decodes gzip and deflate itself, also raw deflate and two stacked codings, which is more than the server's loader accepts; each stage is held to that size); a text holds a percent-encoded run longer than 4,096 characters or still encoded after 8 decodings (a store URL encoded once as a whole fits; deeper nesting used to be decoded at a cost that grew with the square of its length); `ASSETS_BASE_URL` is not clean: it must start with a lower-case `http://` or `https://` and have no query, fragment, userinfo, backslash, dot segment, empty path segment or trailing slash, in its normalised form (a relative, protocol-relative, `https:/host` or `?rev=` base stops it; start-up validation of the variable is unchanged); a database value (a row's data, the settings, an avatar) is larger than the read limit; more than 4,096 `.js` names are not in the store listing; a kept blueprint or collection blueprint has a script that is not a store file for certain. Only `asset://<name>` is one, or a URL that starts with a lower-case `http://` or `https://` (what both the runtime mapping and the server's loader take as remote), that the runtime leaves unchanged, with no userinfo and no `?`, `#`, `%`, backslash or `.`/`..` segment in its text, and whose normalised form is directly under `ASSETS_BASE_URL`. A script hosted elsewhere, a page path such as `/assets/x.js` or `blob/../assets/x.js` (a proxy may serve another application there), a spelling the runtime rewrites (`https:/host/x` becomes `https://https/host/x`, a tab in the scheme, `HTTPS://`, which the server's loader refuses) or a legacy encoded or query spelling all stop it. Only a value starting with a lower-case `blob:` (what `URL.createObjectURL` returns, and what the runtime leaves alone) is no script; `Blob:443/app.js` is fetched from a host named blob, so it stops the clean-up too.
  - **Assumptions.** The server process is the only writer of the store, the trash and the database while it starts (uploads are not accepted until cleaning has finished; other processes changing those files at the same moment are not supported). Database tables are read whole, as the world itself reads them when it starts; the read limit applies to each value, not to a table's total. The assets URL serves each store file's own bytes: a redirect, a rewrite or a cache in front of it that answers a file's URL with something else is not supported (the clean-up reads the store, the world loads what the URL serves). With the local store a removal is recoverable for CLEAN_TRASH_DAYS; with S3 it is not (turn on bucket versioning).
  - **The trash.** With `ASSETS=local`, removed files go to `<world>/assets-trash/<UTC date>/<name>` beside the assets directory (the same file system, so a rename; never served, never listed, on the disk the free-space check measures), one log line with the count and the folder. Folders at least `CLEAN_TRASH_DAYS` old by their UTC date (30 by default, so a file can go up to a day sooner than that after it was moved; `0` deletes at once and empties the trash, folders dated in the future included) are removed once discovery is complete, after anything trashed that is named again has come back from its newest copy that is still there (logged). If none of a named file's copies is there any more, that is logged and the trash is not purged that run. Nothing comes back while `ASSETS_BASE_URL` is not clean: the clean-up then stops before it looks. Before the purge, a kept file that is also trashed must still be in the store; if another process removed it, its trashed copy comes back, and if that fails the trash is not purged. With `ASSETS=s3` objects are deleted: turn on bucket versioning to keep a way back.
  - **Dry run.** `CLEAN=dryrun` changes nothing and names what would be removed (up to 50), what would come back from the trash, and which trash folders would be removed.
  - **No longer a stop.** A prop holding a non-string `url` (props accept any JSON) used to disable cleaning for the whole world; it is now just text.
  - **Cost.** Linear in the text: two walks over it without copying it, a percent-encoded run decoded at most 8 times and only up to 4,096 characters, every file read through one handle and counted up to the limit above, each place that holds an undecidable run recorded once (the log names the first 20). A world of 5,000 files and a 50 MB corpus is cleaned in under a second; 256 KB of nested encoding, or 40,000 places with such runs, stop it in a fraction of a second. The database tables are read whole (see Assumptions).
- wallet: the WalletConnect "Open HashPort Wallet" link opens the app with the connection request.
  - **Before.** HashPort's universal link (https://link.wallet.hashport.com) did not reach the app on an iPhone. Its page sent the player to the App Store, and the pairing link was lost.
  - **Pairing.** HashPort Wallet now pairs through its app scheme, `expo2025-wallet://wc?uri=…`, which is the link Reown's AppKit uses on phones. Safari asks before opening it. Without the app installed, Safari says it cannot open the page.
  - **Unchanged.** MetaMask and Myna Wallet keep their universal links.
- wallet: "Open wallet" (while a signature or tip waits) opens the connected app through an app scheme whenever one is known. It used to try universal links first, which can end on the App Store; Myna Wallet's bare `…/deeplink` base is not one of its app links.
  - **Link order.** The wallet's own app scheme comes first. Next is the scheme of the listed wallet the player tapped, used only when the connected wallet sent no link or one on that wallet's host. Last is the wallet's own universal link, so a stored session whose wallet sends only a universal link still uses it.
  - **Same tab.** A listed wallet's scheme opens in place, where a new tab would stay behind empty. Any other link the wallet sent opens in a new tab.
  - **Refused links.** Android `intent:` and `android-app:` links from a wallet are refused, since their fallback can be any web page.
  - **Forgetting the tapped wallet.** Copy link and Show QR forget the wallet the player tapped, since another wallet may take the link.
- client: on iPhone and iPad, model images load as `<img>` elements in every browser, as iPhone Chrome already did. Safari and Brave got ImageBitmaps from three's GLTFLoader, which WebKit cannot release when memory runs low. With a world's textures, iOS then closed the page ("A problem repeatedly occurred"), most often while the player was in a wallet app. In WebKit under simulated memory pressure, the production world kept about 2.5 GB with ImageBitmaps and about 1.6 GB with `<img>` elements. Desktop browsers and Android keep three's own choice.
- physics: static rigidbodies return zero from velocity-at-position queries and ignore force-at-position calls without entering dynamic-body native functions. Invalid controller descriptors and failed controller allocation leave physics disabled with one warning; teleport still updates the node position. Query spheres reuse one native geometry; the material cache uses unambiguous keys and is capped at 4096 entries, falling back to the default material with a bounded warning at the cap or on allocation failure.
- server: `Server.tick` keeps running after ordinary exceptions, with bounded diagnostics, and never re-arms after shutdown. Fatal physics failures stop the current tick and later tick entry.
- server: revisioned, serialized saves fix a pre-existing lost-edit race when an edit or deletion arrives during an in-flight write. Saves retain the latest settled app record during moves/uploads, record deletions before teardown, and exclude script state before cloning or serialization. The fatal flush includes the latest settings and spawn values within its shared deadline.
- physics: joints work again with genuine rigidbody proxies; forged body references throw. Fixed/socket/hinge/distance joints are covered between two dynamic bodies.
- apps: `getAngularVelocity` returns its vector; `getQuaternion` without an actor returns `(0,0,0,1)`; velocity-at-position getters default their output target. `addForceAtPos`/`addForceAtLocalPos` now forward the requested force mode (scripts passing `impulse` now receive impulse behavior); mode lookup ignores inherited keys. Corrected the position/torque argument messages in `addForceAtPos`, `addForceAtLocalPos`, `addTorque` and `getLocalVelocityAtLocalPos`.
- apps: controller teleport before mount sets the future mount position without throwing; grounded/ceiling flags before the first move are false. Raycasts with a null max distance now reach distant objects. Invalid local player teleports are atomic; direct local pushes reject non-finite components while retaining the existing relay-bound distinction.
- physics: mesh cooking packs decoded XYZ attributes into correctly sized host buffers, handles normalized/interleaved attributes and index widths, refreshes heap views after allocation, and cleans up failed allocations/cooks. Failed native mesh/shape handles are neither cached nor dereferenced; failed shape creation releases newly acquired resources, including Prim's unregistered actor.
- network: a mover who has lost builder rank may only release its own move/upload; transforms, state and other non-release fields are dropped, retaining the last accepted app transform.
- core: scaled anchors yield finite, normalised player poses on both local and remote players; invalid/singular anchor matrices leave the previous pose unchanged. LooseOctree and SnapOctree park non-finite or out-of-budget items without growing the tree beyond its `1e8` root-size budget. Parked items still render, cannot be picked through the octree, and recover on a valid move. Image picking now follows transform changes, including recovery from parking.
- particles: distance emission is capped at 1000 attempts per update, with accumulated remainder cleared on a cap or non-finite arithmetic. Invalid/negative `rateOverDistance` assignments are ignored.
- server: aborted or failed connection setup cleans up its player and socket state; handler and refusal logs contain bounded metadata only.
- core: blueprint and app entity changes are announced before applying them, so events from a synchronous cached rebuild follow the announcement; optimistic upload rebuilds retain the bounded next-version window.
- core: event versions must be non-negative safe integers; fractional future versions cannot bypass builder-only event reservations. Blueprint arrays retain undefined slots as null, policy refusals show a toast, and script sends consistently return false on refusal. Engine player cleanup uses system output; late packets are logged as disconnected.
- apps: `player.setSessionAvatar()` called from a client script on a remote player cleared that player's avatar instead of changing it (the relayed packet had the wrong shape); the remote path is now server-only (see Changed).
- server: a chat message from a client is rebuilt from its text only (trimmed, up to 500 characters; sender and time stamped by the server). A message whose body was not text used to stay in the history and break the chat list of every client that joined afterwards; it is now refused. The client also skips a non-text body when rendering or showing a bubble.
- server: a flood of `entityEvent` packets could grow the heap without bound: every packet was decoded on arrival whatever its size, an app queued events without limit while it rebuilt, one event with a far-future blueprint version at the head of that queue held back every later event for ever, and a `NaN` or `Infinity` version passed the type check. All four are closed (see Changed).
- apps: `setSessionAvatar('')` on the local player clears the session avatar again, as it did before the avatar URL policy.
- server: a user's name, avatar and rank could differ between the session and the database, and a reload could bring an old value back (C9, part A). Each change was a write of its own, a failed one was logged and forgotten, and a join took the row as it read it. On PostgreSQL (a pool of 2 to 10 connections) two writes of one user could land in the other order, and a join could read the row before a write landed and keep the old rank or name for its whole session. On SQLite (production: one connection, writes in the order made) both needed a write that failed. This is correctness work of modest priority, not the closing of an attack.
  - **What the server decided is kept until the database has it** (`src/server/userRows.js`). The server is the only writer of the `users` table, so for a user it has changed it knows the values it decided. They are written one statement per user at a time, in the order decided; a change made while a statement is under way goes out with the next one (five names taken in one tick are two statements: the first, then the last). No handler waits for the write any more.
  - **A join** puts what is kept for its user over the row it read, after its last wait and with nothing awaited before the player is added; what is kept is taken as what the row holds. A rank an admin took, or a name the player set, before that moment is in the new session whether its write is still on its way, landed after the row was read, or failed; one made after it finds the player in the world. A name the connection itself brings is applied after it, as before (see Still as before). The values are kept for as long as the user is joining, so the takeover of a stale connection no longer waits for rank writes (up to 2 s, a wait the newcomer's closing did not end) and no longer reads the row a second time.
  - **A write that fails** is logged once per user, and again only after that user's writes have gone through without a failure in between (`[network] <columns> of user "<id>" not stored ...`). Nothing tries again by itself. The values stay in memory, joins of that user get them, and they are written again with the user's next change and once at the final save. When a statement that carried several columns fails, each column is written once on its own, so a value the database refuses (too long for its column on PostgreSQL) does not keep a rank from being stored.
  - **Token grants.** A rank an admin gives or takes, and the admin code, end that session's token grant when the rank is decided: the mark and the wallet's entry go in the same turn, whatever the rank and whatever becomes of its write. What the server decided is the user's rank for as long as it runs, so whether a builder rank is a token's or an admin's does not depend on the database. (The grant used to end when a write landed while the session had the rank written. With writes of different ranks overlapping and one failing, that went wrong both ways: builder, visitor, builder with the last write failing left a builder rank that the database did not hold and that no wallet disconnect took; builder landing before a failed visitor let the gate grant again, and a wallet disconnect then took a rank that the next reload gave back.)
    - A decided rank is not taken by a wallet disconnect or a new sign-in from the moment it is decided, also while its write is on its way and after it failed.
    - A token grants builder again whenever the decided rank is below it and the balance is enough, also while a rank of that user is being written (the gate used to answer "Could not check your balance" until the write had settled). A wallet disconnect then takes that grant and leaves the decided rank.
    - A write that fails changes nothing in the session: it and every later join of that user have the decided rank for as long as this server runs. A server started later has the database's rank: a promotion the database never took is gone after a restart, and a demotion it never took is undone.
  - **Final save.** The writes under way are waited for and what a failed write left unstored is tried once more for each user, under the same deadline as the rest of the save and with no statement started after it. What the database still has not taken is counted: `users=N` in the `not persisted` line, and the process ends with 1.
  - **What this does not give.** Durability: a crash, a kill, or a database that refuses to the end loses the changes it had not taken, and after a restart the rows are what the database held. A repair of a write that can never succeed: it is tried again with each later change of that user and fails again. Agreement with a hand edit: while the server runs, a value it decided overwrites an edited one at its next write of that column, and joins get the server's value; edit the `users` table only with the server stopped. One statement across tables: a user's row and the world's other tables are still written apart.
  - **Still as before.** A rank changed in the moment between a player's old connection leaving and the new one's player being added finds no player: it is not made, and the admin is not told. A name a connection brings itself (the `name` query parameter of its WebSocket URL; the browser client sends none) is that session's name, also over a name the player set on the old connection while the new one was joining; it is not written to the row, so a later join without it gets the name that was set.
- client, agent: what is made is let go of, and a few leftovers (P13: NODE-08, CSYS-08, CSYS-09, CSYS-12, CSYS-17, UI-10).
  - **Video sources.** A video with several sources (`linked` set to a key, or to `false`) gave every source the wait and the dimensions of the first one: a later video went on before its own element had any data, and when the first source was unmounted before it had loaded, the later ones waited for good. Each source now waits for its own element. A video that cannot be loaded (a missing file, one the browser cannot decode, an HLS stream the player gives up on) ends that wait: the node stays as it was, showing its colour, and the console says `[video] not loaded: ...`; it used to wait for good without a word. The HLS player of a source is destroyed with it (its loaders, buffers and worker stayed), and a source that was let go is no longer unmuted by the first click afterwards.
  - **Audio and voice.** The page's click, touch and key listeners that unlock audio stayed after the audio system was destroyed. A voice server that cannot be reached, or that refuses the token, was a rejection nobody handled (before the world is ready the page counts one as a failed start); it is logged, and voice stays unconnected, as it did.
  - **A world that is destroyed** (`world.destroy()`; the agent in `agent.mjs` makes a new world for each connection, the browser never destroys its world). Nothing released the physics of the old world: its scene, with its buffers and callbacks, stayed in the PhysX heap, about 530 allocations for each world the agent had been in, and so did the local player's body, shape and material. `Physics.destroy()` now gives back what `init()` allocated, and the local player has a `destroy()` that takes its body out of the scene and releases it, releases its control and leaves the frame loop. Measured on the real PhysX build, after the first few worlds (PhysX's own pools grow on first use): ten worlds made, stepped and destroyed leave the count of live allocations where it was. The node client's tick is not scheduled again when the world was destroyed from inside it. The graphics, environment, stats, nametags, particles and audio systems take their listeners off the world, the prefs and the settings when destroyed; graphics threw there when it had never been initialised. `World.destroy()` goes on to every system when one of them throws (it stopped there, and the systems after it, physics among them, were not destroyed); the first failure is thrown at the end, and the others are logged with the system's name.
  - **What `Physics.destroy()` does not release.** The PxPhysics object: every world of a process is handed the same one, and the meshes cooked for the process belong to it. And the scene itself when something is still registered in it at that point (a body or a controller nobody unmounted): it is kept, as before, with one warning, because a controller that is still held would be freed under its holder. A destroyed system does nothing in a frame that still runs, finds nothing in a query, and refuses a body that mounts with an error. A step under way ends when a contact or trigger callback destroys the world (it went on into the released scene: a native trap, or a fatal physics failure reported); a body left in a kept scene is no longer moved or snapped through its handle (a move threw on a freed transform) and can still be let go of; and a system destroyed while it still waited for PhysX to load makes nothing when the load ends (it made a whole scene that nothing released).
  - **A local player removed while it loads.** A player removed while its set-up still waited for the first assets was set up when the wait ended: a body in the scene, a bound control, a place in the frame loop, and `ready` told to the world. The set-up now stops.
  - **Dropped files and links.** A link dropped on the world was fetched and taken as the file whatever the answer: an error page became `thing.glb`, and a body of any size was read into memory before the size check a dropped file gets. An answer that is not a success is refused, and so is one larger than the upload limit, by its declared length before it is read, and otherwise while it is read (reading stops at the chunk that passes the limit; with no limit known, nothing is refused for its size, as before). A drop that fails (a link that cannot be fetched, a `.hyp` file that is not one) was a rejection nobody handled; it is logged and a toast says "That could not be added.".
  - **`.hyp` files** (`importApp`, also read by the server for its collections). The header was taken as it came. An asset `size` that was negative, not a number or past the end of the file cut the assets from the wrong bytes: alternating `+n` and `-n` gave every asset the same bytes again, each one a copy of the payload. A file is now refused with a reason unless its header has a blueprint and a list of assets, each with a `url` and a whole `size`, lying one after another within the file. The five files shipped with the world import as before. On the server, a file a collection's manifest names that is refused this way is left out of that collection, with `[collections] <collection>: <file> is left out: <reason>` in the log, and the start goes on with the other apps and collections (before, some of these loaded with assets cut from the wrong bytes, and others stopped the start); a file that cannot be read, or a manifest that cannot be, still stops the start. The scene app a new world's database is given from `src/world/scene.hyp` (the code's own file, read once by a migration) is not left out: a refusal there stops the start. What the blueprint says is not checked here; it goes through the blueprint policy where it is used.
  - **Editor.** The script editor left its watcher on the app when it was closed: the next change of the script called `setValue` on the disposed editor. The curve editor added a chart for every curve it was shown and took none out.
  - **Looked at and left.** A video source released more often than it was taken: the count goes below zero, but the source was already cleaned up at zero and is not reachable afterwards, so nothing follows from it; no floor was added. `Physics.raycast` returns one shared hit object: its two callers read it before the next raycast (the script API copies it), so it stays. A model's `getEntity(instanceId)`, which logged "TODO: remove if you dont ever see this", is removed with its binding on the instanced mesh: every raycast goes through the octree, whose items carry their own lookup, and nothing called it. The gizmo's local/world key used a bitwise `&` between two booleans, which gives the same result as `&&`; it reads `&&` now.

### Security
- scripts, chat: **an app line the server relays from a visitor's connection now names that visitor** (OD-6). A client script's broadcast `world.chat(msg, true)` reaches the others as a line of the app (`appChat`, relayed by the server), and nothing on the line said whose connection sent it. Client scripts run in each visitor's browser, so a modified client could post any text under the name of any app in the world, with any display name ("Admin"), and the line looked like one the app's server script wrote.
  - **What the line shows now.** The server sets `via: { id, name }` on every line it relays from a connection: the id and the name that the sending connection's player has on the server when the line arrives. Nothing of it comes from the packet: an `appChat` packet's own `via`, or any other field it adds, is not read, and a player's `chatAdded` packet is always made a `player` line, which has no `via`. The chat shows it after the app's name and the optional `[from]` as a smaller, dimmer `via <name>` (`via Anonymous` when the name is empty), with the hint (`title`) "Relayed from Alice's connection (player id: …)" for a visitor named Alice; the player id, cut to 64 characters, tells two visitors of one name apart, and a `via` whose id is not text gets the hint without the parenthesis. The sender's own client shows the same on its own copy, except a copy made before its own player is known (only a script that runs while the join snapshot is still being read can make one): that copy has none and shows without the suffix, and the others' copies have the server's `via` all the same. A server script's line has no `via`, nor has a client script's line that is not broadcast; an app line without one is drawn as before (the same markup; the style sheet has one rule more, for `.message-via`, so its generated class name changes). The `via` of the server's stored chat and of the `chat` event is frozen; a newcomer's join snapshot carries it too, as a plain object (packing does not keep the freezing, and `Chat.deserialize` keeps the snapshot's messages as they come).
  - **What it does not prove.** It is an attribution, not an authentication. It says which visitor's connection relayed the line, not what wrote it: a modified client can still post any text under the name of any app in the world, with any display name (see "What remains" in the `world.chat()` entry below). The name in `via` is the one that visitor chose, as trustworthy as the name on their own chat lines and no more; two visitors can have the same name, and the player id in the hint (or `via.id`, for a script) tells them apart. The id is the player's on the server, the same across that user's reconnections, not a mark of one browser session. A line without `via` is not thereby a server script's: a client script's line that is not broadcast has none, and neither has a line from an older server. What changes is that every reader sees which visitor's connection relayed the line.
  - **The app's name** on a relayed line is read from the server's blueprint record that the app names when the line arrives (`world.blueprints`, by the app's `blueprint` id), no longer from the app's last finished build (`App.blueprint`, set only once its model and script have loaded). A line that arrived before the server's own build of that app had loaded, which a client script that speaks as soon as it starts can send, carried an empty name, shown as "App" (seen in `fullstack.mjs` B4h, on both engines); while an app rebuilds after its blueprint changed, the line had the old record's name. It now carries the current record's name; a record that does not exist gives `''`, shown as "App", as before. The name says which app the line was posted under, at the time of the relay; it establishes nothing about which script, or which version of it, sent the line.
  - **Policy.** `chatEnvelope` (`src/core/extras/chatPolicy.js`, which every line goes through when it is added, on the server and on each client) keeps `via` on an app line only, as a frozen `{ id, name }`: the id text cut to 64 characters, the name as a player's name (`sanitizeName`; `''` when it has none), each read once, nothing else under it. A `via` that is not a plain object, has no text id, or throws when read (a getter, a Proxy whose trap throws) is dropped, and on any other kind of line it is dropped unread; a Proxy that answers is read once per value and copied. A client does not put the chat of its join snapshot through the envelope (as before, for every field): the server did, for each line, when it was added, and the chat panel reads `via` through the same text bounds as the rest of a line.
  - **Join snapshot.** Chat history is not counted by the join budget: it is dropped from a snapshot that would not fit (`joinBudget.js`, the `chat` step), as before. The largest `via` (an id of 64 characters and a name of 64 three-byte characters) adds about 275 bytes to a relayed line and about 13 KB to the 50 lines a snapshot keeps (12,885 to 13,065 bytes in two measurements with the server's packer; the figure moves with the other fields). The worst-case join test now has a full chat of such lines.
  - **Protocol.** The server's `chatAdded` gains the field; `PROTOCOL_VERSION` stays 2. A client or node agent of an older build drops it (its envelope keeps the fields it knows) and shows the line as before.
  - **Bundled apps.** No app of the default collection or the scene, and no example in `docs/` or `examples/`, calls `world.chat()` with `broadcast` from a client script, so nothing they show changes. A world's own client scripts that broadcast lines now show each with the suffix.
  - **Tests.** `test/chatPolicy.test.js`: the envelope keeps a frozen copy of `{ id, name }` on an app line, within its bounds; a `via` that is a string, a list, a `Map`, a class instance, a boxed string, a Proxy whose trap throws, or has a getter that throws keeps none; each value is read once, also through a Proxy that answers; on every other kind it is dropped unread. `test/scriptChat.test.js`: the server's stamp comes from the connection's player whatever the packet's `via` is (an object naming someone else, a string, a list, a getter, a Proxy: none of them is read), and follows a rename; the stored line, the `chat` event (whose `via` a listener cannot change), the relayed packet and the snapshot as a client reads them; a player's `chatAdded` and a server script's line have none; the sender's own copy, a line that is not broadcast, and a copy made before the local player is known; the app's name with the real network class and blueprint records: an app whose build has not loaded, a record modified while the app rebuilds, an app moved to another record, a record that does not exist; the chat panel's markup with `via` (the suffix, the hint with and without the player id, the id's bound, `Anonymous`, hostile names and ids, each value read once) and without it, compared with the markup recorded before the change. 30 hand mutants each fail a test (among them: the server reading the packet's `via`, the stamp taking the packet's `from` as the name, the envelope keeping `via` on a player line or other keys under it, the panel dropping the suffix or the player id, the hint back to a wording that names the app's script, the local copy without `via`, the app's name read from the finished build again). End to end: `access-control.mjs` [app-chat] (no browser): a visitor's lines under another app's name, claiming a display name and a `via` of their own, reach the others and a newcomer's snapshot with that visitor's `via`, and a server script's line has none; `fullstack.mjs` B4h on Chromium and WebKit: the line a browser's client script sends as soon as it starts reaches the others with the app's name and the browser's player in `via`; in the browser, the suffix and its hint (with the player id) on the browser's own line and on a wire client's line under the same app (the wire client's name and id, whatever `via` it claimed), none on a server script's line.
- server, build: **`/env.js` and the page's bundles now carry a fixed list of names, not every `PUBLIC_` variable** (P14). Until now the prefix alone decided: whatever an operator named `PUBLIC_…` was served to every visitor in `/env.js` and, when set at build time, written into the downloadable bundles (one name was refused, `PUBLIC_RPC_URL`). A secret named that way by mistake was public.
  - **What is served.** In `/env.js`, when they are set: the eight names the client code reads (`PUBLIC_WS_URL`, `PUBLIC_PLAYER_COLLISION`, `PUBLIC_DEBUG`, `PUBLIC_TOKEN_MINT`, `PUBLIC_REQUIRED_TOKEN_AMOUNT`, `PUBLIC_EVM_CHAIN_IDS`, `PUBLIC_EVM_TIP_TOKENS`, `PUBLIC_WALLETCONNECT_PROJECT_ID`), and three it served before and still serves although nothing in this repository reads them from the page (`PUBLIC_API_URL`, `PUBLIC_MAX_UPLOAD_SIZE`, `PUBLIC_TOKEN_DECIMALS`: the server reads them; a page or tool outside this repository may read them there). The two lists are in `src/core/extras/publicEnv.js`. The build's define map holds the eight only (`scripts/lib/buildDefines.mjs`): no bundled code reads the other three.
  - **What changes for a world.** An unknown `PUBLIC_` name is no longer served: a `PUBLIC_` variable that is set and is on neither list is left out of `/env.js` and of the build's define map, and named once in an error line when the server starts (`[envs] not handed to the page (/env.js): …`: names, never values). The server starts all the same. A world that sets no other `PUBLIC_` name gets the same `/env.js` as before, to the byte. `PUBLIC_RPC_URL` stays out, as before, and is not named in that line. To have the client read a new name, add it to the list and read it by its full name.
  - **What this does not do.** It goes by names and never looks at a value: a secret put under one of the eleven served names is served. Tests hold the first list to the `PUBLIC_` names written in the files that are bundled for a browser and the second to exactly those three, and fail when browser code indexes the environment by anything but a string (`test/publicEnv.test.js`, `test/buildDefines.test.js`); a name put together at run time in some other way would not be seen by them.
- wallet (Solana): three settings of the token gate no longer grant builder access by accident (P12: N4, N5, DOC-03; CHAIN-01 and LARP-02 only in part). The gate itself is as weak as before: see "Not changed".
  - **An empty amount made every wallet a builder.** `PUBLIC_REQUIRED_TOKEN_AMOUNT=` (the line left empty) read as 0, and a wallet without a token account has a known balance of 0: every visitor who signed with any wallet became a builder. A negative amount did the same, and a value that is not a number (`100,000`) fell back to 10,000 without a word. The amount is now a number above 0 (`src/core/extras/tokenRequirement.js`; 10,000 when the variable is absent). Anything else: wallets are still verified and scripts still read balances, nobody is granted builder access by token, the chain is not asked for it, the visitor is told in chat and in the wallet panel, and the server logs an error at start-up. The server checks the amount again where it grants.
  - **No RPC URL meant the public devnet node.** Without `SOLANA_RPC_URL` (and `PUBLIC_RPC_URL`) the server asked `api.devnet.solana.com`: another chain, whose accounts for the mint are not the ones the world means, read as the world's balances. Now no node is asked, every balance is unknown, nothing is granted, and the server logs an error at start-up.
  - **A new sign-in kept the old rank.** A wallet sign-in started on a connection takes the earlier verification away at once, but the builder rank granted on it stayed: a session without a verified wallet kept building. The grant now ends when the new sign-in is accepted (a request refused by the 5 s cooldown changes nothing) and comes back when the new signature is verified and the balance of that wallet is read. A stored rank is not touched.
  - **`.env.example`.** `PUBLIC_TOKEN_MINT` is empty (it held Coo World's mint: a copied file gated on a token its world never chose, against devnet). Four lines nothing reads are gone, one of which asked for a wallet's private key (`SOLANA_PKEY_ARRAY`, `SOLANA_WATCH_ADDRESS`, `SOLANA_WITHDRAWAL_FEE_BPS`, `SOLANA_FEE_WALLET_ADDRESS`); `test/envExample.test.js` holds every line of the file to a name the source uses as a whole word outside its line comments (a floor, not a proof that the value is read).
  - **A stored rank marked as a grant.** When two administrative writes of the same rank for one player overlapped, the first to land left the session marked as token-granted (`persistRank` gave up because the revision had moved), and ending "the grant" (a wallet disconnect, or now a new sign-in) then took a builder rank the database holds until the next reload. That change made a write that lands while the session still has the rank it wrote end the grant. The rule is replaced by the entry on users' rows under Fixed: a rank an admin gives ends the grant when it is given, not when a write lands.
  - **Not changed.** The balance is read when the grant is made and not again: a wallet that sells or moves its tokens stays a builder until its session ends, the same tokens can grant a second wallet, and the "wallet disconnected" report comes from the page, so a changed page does not send it. What a builder made stays. The RPC node is believed. The README now says all of this under "What ends a grant, and what does not".
  - **Upgrading.** A world that runs the token gate must have `SOLANA_RPC_URL` (or `PUBLIC_RPC_URL`) set and a `PUBLIC_REQUIRED_TOKEN_AMOUNT` that is a number above 0 or absent; otherwise no wallet is granted builder access after this change. Both cases are an error line in the server log at start-up.
- server: any builder could start any number of AI actions at once, without end, each one or two requests on the operator's provider account (only the Anthropic client set an output limit); where every visitor is a builder (a world without an admin code), anyone could. Nothing counted them and the log did not say who asked. Actions now have a budget per builder and for the world and a deadline, every provider request has an output limit, and each started action is logged with what it is and who it is counted against. In a world open to all, user ids are free, so there only the world's limits hold: at the defaults, 60 actions an hour, at most 120 provider requests before the SDKs' own retries.
- client: the builder's number fields no longer run what is typed into them (P14).
  - **What it was.** A number field (a prop of type number, a position, rotation or scale axis) handed its text to `eval`, to let `1.5 * 2` be typed. Anything else typed or pasted there ran as well, in the page, with the page's session and its wallet connections: "paste this into the position field" was enough.
  - **Now.** The text is read as arithmetic and never run: numbers, `+ - * / %` and `**`, parentheses, `PI` (also `pi`, `Math.PI`), `E`, and `abs ceil floor round sqrt sin cos tan min max pow` (with or without `Math.`). Anything else leaves the field at the value it had, as a mistyped entry did before. A result that is not a finite number (`1 / 0`) is refused too; it used to be stored as `Infinity` or `NaN`.
  - **An entry that is not taken changes nothing.** The field shows its value again, nothing is written, and the console says why (`[field] the entry is not taken: …`). A number field used to write its value back rounded to the digits it shows (a stored `0.004` in a field of two digits became `0`); a three-axis field threw (the whole vector was put in the axis' place). A step with the arrow keys that would leave the field's range changes nothing either, and neither does leaving a field in which nothing was typed (clicking into a field and out again used to write the rounded value too).
  - **Changed for builders.** Other JavaScript that happened to work in these fields (`Math.random()`, hex numbers, other functions of `Math`) is no longer read.
- server: `/status` (no authentication, readable from any origin) listed every connected player's user id, name and live position. It now gives a count. A client that joins the world still receives the players in its snapshot; what is gone is the list for anyone who merely asks over HTTP.
- server: Node.js 22.23.4 → 22.23.5 (Docker image, `.nvmrc`, engines): the security release of 2026-11-11. Of its sixteen fixes, none of the seven rated High is reachable in this server as it is built: they are in `http2` (not used: the server speaks HTTP/1.1 only), in TLS servers (it makes none: it listens in cleartext, and TLS ends in whatever is put in front of it) and in `https` servers (CVE-2026-123864 reads other connections' memory only over `node:https`; the advisory says cleartext `node:http` is not affected). The outbound `https.Agent` fix needs requests that differ in their `ca` option (ours never set one), the proxy-credentials fix a proxy configured from the environment, and the Permission Model fixes `--permission`. What applies without a named CVE is the bundled `llhttp` update: the parser of every request this server receives. Taken as routine hygiene, not as an emergency.
- server: a session token was the account for ever: no end, and no way to change the signing secret without resetting every identity. Tokens now end (see Changed) and the secret can be rotated with `JWT_SECRET_PREVIOUS`. Renewal does not cancel a token that leaked; only a new secret does. The signing algorithm is pinned to HS256 on verification (the library already refused unsigned tokens for a string secret; the pin also refuses the other HMAC sizes).
- apps: a script could read the wallet address of any EVM-verified visitor. `player.getEvmHolding()` asked whatever contract the script named, on any chain with a server RPC, and gave back the number that contract answered for the player's verified address. A script author deployed a contract whose `balanceOf(owner, id)` returned 40 bits of the owner's address for each `id`, and four calls (ids 0 to 3) gave the whole address, of every verified visitor of a world running that script, not only of players who receive tips. `docs/scripting/world/Player.md` and the two example apps said the address never reaches the script.
  - **Now.** The server makes a holdings read only for a contract the operator named in the new `EVM_HOLDING_CONTRACTS` (comma-separated `chainId:0xaddress`, compared without regard to case). For any other contract the script gets `known: false`, the same answer as for a chain without an RPC, the node is not asked, and the server log names the contract once (the first 16 unlisted contracts of a server run, then one line saying there are more; never a player's address).
  - **The default.** With the variable not set, the list is one contract: OpenPay's license contract on Polygon, `137:0x1c652241Cc820Ce897F0d8D9351Df9AD8a0916cc`, so the shipped NFT door works as before. Setting the variable replaces that default (it does not add to it); setting it to nothing allows no contract. An entry in another form stops the server at start-up with an `[envs]` error instead of running with part of a list. The start-up log says which contracts are listed.
  - **What changes for a world.** A script that asks about any other contract (the `nft-gate.js` example set to an NFT of your own, for one) now gets `known: false` every time, and its sign says to try again, until the operator lists that contract and restarts the server.
  - **What this is not.** The list bounds which contracts are asked, not what they answer. Listing a contract trusts its `balanceOf` / `ownerOf` not to encode the owner: a listed contract that is upgradeable, or that its operator controls, can still return address bits, and nothing checks a listed contract again. A listed ERC-721 answers `ownerOf` for every token id, so a script that asks id by id learns which token a visitor holds and with it the wallet (one read per question against the shared budget, which slows this and does not stop it). A balance of a token with few holders can name a wallet. Every script in the world can ask about every listed contract. `getTokenBalance()` (the Solana balance, readable by client scripts) and the addresses that a tip announcement lets anyone find on chain are as they were.
  - **Docs.** `Player.md` has a section on what a script can learn from a holdings check; the comments of `nft-gate.js` and `nft-door.js`, the README and `.env.example` say the same.
- server: one visitor could get other players disconnected, and stall the server. Every packet shares one budget per connection (about a thousand small packets a second), which bounds the work of reading them, not what each sets off. A chat line is written to every connection: a thousand a second fill the write backlog of every recipient on a slow link within seconds, and a recipient whose backlog is full is closed. A name change is written to every connection and to the database: with SQLite each is a commit on the server's own thread.
  - **Budgets for what a person does by hand** (`src/core/extras/actionBudget.js`), per connection, counted by when the packet arrived: chat 1 a second (6 at once), a client script's chat 2 (10), the player's own name, avatar and session avatar 1 every 2 seconds (5), commands 2 (10), world settings and the spawn 5 (30), and kicks, mutes and changes of rank 2 (10), in a bucket of their own so that editing settings never leaves a moderator without one. A request that would be refused anyway (a player who is not here, a value the rules refuse, an admin's setting from a builder, an invalid transform for the spawn) is checked first and uses no budget. The admin code keeps its own brake and is not counted with commands; a granted `/admin` is counted as a change of rank. What goes over a budget is not carried out when it arrives:
    - a line of chat, a command, a spawn, a kick, a mute or a change of rank is dropped; the sender is told once per wait where a person can get there (chat, `/name`, commands, the spawn, ranks).
    - a name, avatar or session avatar is taken out of its packet, whose other fields (movement) go on, and the sender gets back the value the server keeps. A name the server shortened is corrected as before, whatever became of the avatar in the same packet.
    - a world setting is held, not dropped: the latest value of each setting from that connection is applied when the budget allows, and checked again then (the sender's rank may have changed). A number field sends a change per key repeat, about thirty a second with an arrow key held: the value the builder ends on is the one the world keeps, the sender's field is not set back under the key, and the others are told the first thirty and then at most five a second. Of the values of one setting held for several builders, only the one that came last can be applied: the others wait, and are let go when it is applied (or applied after all, on a later tick, if it goes first: its builder leaves, loses the rank, or it is refused when checked again). A held value refused when its turn comes (its builder lost the rank) is not applied, and its sender alone is told the value the world keeps. So between a value's arrival and its application no other value of that setting is applied, and the pane of a builder whose values are held ends on the value the world keeps. Not covered, as before the budgets: a value sent by someone who is no longer a builder is refused as it comes, without an answer; and two changes crossing in flight, where the builder whose change reached the server second is told the first one and, when that message arrives after its own change, shows it while the world keeps its own. Nothing is held for a connection that is gone, or once the server stops.
  - **A chat line cannot close a recipient.** Player and app chat are written as skippable: a connection whose write backlog is high misses the line instead of being closed for it.
  - **An effect is seven fields.** A player's effect (`ef`) was any JSON up to 8 KB, written to everyone with each update; it is `anchorId`, `emote`, `snare`, `freeze`, `turn`, `duration` and `cancellable`, each a short primitive, and at most 2 KB in all (the UTF-8 bytes of its JSON; what the engine builds is under 1 KB in ASCII). One with another field, or over that size, is not an effect.
  - **Nothing new, nothing done.** A name or avatar equal to the one the player has is not told to anyone or stored again. Each change that is taken is one write to the database, as before: the identity budget is what bounds how many there are.
  - **What this is not.** The budgets are per connection: several connections multiply them, and the limits on connections per address are what bounds that. Movement, building and script events are not budgeted here (an honest client sends them at frame rate, and a dropped one leaves players out of step): a flood of those still reaches everyone. Writes of a user's row (name, avatar, rank) are as they were: one UPDATE for each change, not ordered or merged per user, and a player who joins again reads the row as it is at that moment. The spawn is not merged either: each change of it that the budget lets through is written, also one that a later change replaced while it waited for its turn. Chat is limited in rate, not in content.
- server: wrong admin codes cost nothing. `/admin <code>` compared with `===`, and a wrong code was neither counted, delayed, answered nor logged, at up to about a thousand attempts a second per connection: a short code could be found by trying, unnoticed. Codes are now compared in constant time (SHA-256 digests, `timingSafeEqual`) and wrong ones are slowed down per user id, per address and per wider network, with one summary line in the log a minute after the first wrong code (never the code, never an address). This is a brake, not a wall: every new connection is a new user id, so only addresses hold a guesser, at about 1,400 guesses a day from one IPv4 address and 8,600 from one IPv6 /48. A six-digit code does not hold for long against that; 16 random characters do. Someone on an admin's own address can keep that address waiting; a stored admin rank needs no code.
- server: one visitor could run a world's server scripts about a thousand times a second. A script event from a client (`app.send` in a browser) runs the app's server listener when it arrives, and only the budget every packet shares bounded how many: about a thousand small packets a second per connection. Script events now have a budget of their own, per connection (`event` in `src/core/extras/actionBudget.js`): 320 a second, up to 1,280 at once after a quiet time, counted by when the packet arrived and shared by all the apps the connection sends to. It is asked only for an event the app takes: one its script has a listener for, or one the app keeps while it builds (a builder's event for the next version of a blueprint included). An event that is neither kept nor listened for uses none: one for an app that is not there, of a version the app refuses, with a name the running script does not listen for, or for an app whose script has crashed. An event over the budget is dropped, not held or delayed: it is counted with the app's other dropped events and logged once per app and once per connection. Nobody is closed, and the sending script is not told (`app.send` has returned `true`). The browser keeps the same count on its own clock, of every event it sends, and warns once in the console that events may be dropped. A script that sends an event every frame sends 60 to 240 a second, depending on the display; two apps doing so at 144 Hz send 288, which passes. A connection whose scripts send more than 320 a second for longer than the burst lasts loses the excess: `docs/scripting/app/App.md` said "Send deltas, not full state, at high rates" and now gives the numbers and says to send at a fixed rate. What this is not: a limit per person (each connection has its own budget, and the connection limits allow 8 per address); a bound on large events (one of 6 KiB or more is bounded below 320 a second by the budget every packet shares); a bound on what a listener does with 320 events a second, or on what scripts send. Movement and building are not budgeted, as before.
- server: any visitor could make every other visitor's browser download and parse a file from any host. A player's session avatar and emote took any `http(s)` URL from any connection (`sessionAvatar` in a player's own update or the `avatar` parameter of the `/ws` URL; the emote in `e`), with the Custom Avatars setting on or off, and with it on, the stored avatar took one too and kept it for every later visit. Each client that saw the player fetched the file (up to 64 MiB for an avatar, 32 MiB for an emote): the sender learned every viewer's address and got their parser and their bandwidth. Now a URL outside the world (`isExternalUrl`: anything that, as the loaders read and resolve it, is not requested from the world's own store: an `asset://…` file, or an address under the store's own URL) is taken from a player only while the player is a builder, or when it is the very URL a server script chose for that player (the session avatar a script set, one of the last 16 emotes a script's effects carried). A refused emote becomes none; a refused session avatar is dropped and the sender's own view is set back. What this does not change: builders, and server scripts, can still name any URL; with Custom Avatars on a visitor can still upload a VRM of their own making, which everyone parses (size-capped, served from the world's store, with no references outside its own file). A server script that passes on a URL a visitor supplied gives that visitor the old reach.
- server: one visitor could get every player on a slower link disconnected by moving. What a client changes on its own player (position, facing, emote, effect) was written to every other connection as each packet was handled, and only the budget every packet shares bounded how many: about a thousand a second of 1.6 KB each from one connection, which is about 1.7 MB a second to each recipient (2.7 MB a second as the write backlog counts it, with 1 KiB for each write). A recipient that could not take about 14 Mbit/s fell behind until its backlog reached 1 MiB and was closed, at 8 Mbit/s within about a second, and again when it came back.
  - **A player's own changes are merged per tick** (`src/core/extras/pendingChanges.js`). The server applies each change when its packet is handled, as before, and tells the others once, when the tick's packets have been handled: one packet per player, with the latest value of each field its client changed in that tick (a teleport stays a teleport when the player moved again in the tick). So per connection, at most one own-player packet per server tick is written to each other connection: 30 a second of at most about 3.8 KB of packet (every field at its limit in three-byte characters, since the limits on urls and names count characters; about 2.7 KB in ASCII), which is about 114 KB a second (about 145 KB as the backlog counts it, with 1 KiB for each write; the framing of the connection is not counted in either), and up to about 2.5 KB more in a tick in which the identity budget lets a name or an avatar through. A client sends its movement 8 times a second, so normally one change in a tick, told as it came; two that arrive in one tick are told as one.
  - **What the others see differently.** The packet is written at the end of the tick's packets, not when its own packet was handled: a delay of less than a tick (33 ms), and a line of chat sent after a step in the same tick now arrives before the step. The values in between are not sent: of a client script that teleports its player or sets an effect on every frame the others get what each tick ended on, 30 a second, and an effect set and ended within one tick is never shown to them (`docs/scripting/world/Player.md` says so).
  - **What the server itself tells everyone about a player is not undone by that player's earlier change of the same tick.** An effect, a session avatar or health set by a server script, `/name`, a change of rank and the urls a former builder may no longer show are written at once, as before; their fields are taken out of what is pending for that player, which would otherwise be written after them and set the others back to the older value. A script's write that was refused (the tick's budget for script writes is used up) reached nobody and takes nothing out. A recipient that a script's write skipped (its backlog is over 512 KiB) misses the client's value of that field as well, and stays on an older one until the field changes again. Nothing is told about a player after its removal, nothing is kept from one tick to the next, and a server that is stopping lets go unsent what it had not yet told.
  - **What this is not.** The bound is per connection, not per person: several connections multiply it (8 per address by the connection limits), and it is a bound on what one connection makes the server write, not a promise to a slow recipient: enough players moving at once still fill a link that cannot carry them. The server still applies every packet as it arrives. **Builders are not bounded**: changes to apps and blueprints are written as they come, as before, and one builder can still have about 1.2 MB a second written to every recipient by adding and removing an app in a loop (146 pairs a second of 8.3 KB), or about 2 MB a second with app state over nine or more apps, or with blueprint versions (34 a second of 60 KB); a recipient below about 12 to 16 Mbit/s is closed, again and again. Only a budget of bytes per source would bound that, and there is none. The entry above on budgets for what a person does by hand says movement is not budgeted: it still is not (none of it is refused); it is merged.
- server: ten log lines printed an id or a setting's name from a packet with only its length cut, so a line break in it started a line of the sender's making in the server's log. They print it as `previewValue` does: quoted, printable characters only.
- scripts: a node's `cursor` could be an image (`url(...)`). The value is the cursor style of the page's body while the pointer is over the node, so it also showed over the engine's own interface: a script's image could stand in for the pointer over the wallet, tip and confirmation dialogs, drawn away from where a click lands. Only CSS cursor keywords are passed on (`src/core/extras/cursorKeyword.js`), in the node's setter and again where the style is written. (That the browser fetched the image was not the problem: a script can make a visitor's browser fetch an image through any node that shows one.)
- moderation: a kick kept nobody out and did not end what the kicked player said or showed. The server closed the world's connection and nothing more: the player reloaded and was back as the same user with the same rank, and since the voice room is a connection of its own that no client ever closed, everyone who already heard a kicked (or simply disconnected) player kept hearing them, at their last position, and kept seeing their screen share.
  - **Leaving the world ends voice.** A client leaves the voice room when it is kicked or loses the world's connection, and every client drops a player's voice and screen share when that player leaves the world, whatever the voice server says.
  - **A kick holds for a while** (`src/server/kickList.js`). The kicked user id is refused for `KICK_MINUTES` (default 10, 0 to 1440; 0 only disconnects), with the kick code `kicked`, before anything is added or announced, also when the kick lands while the same user is reconnecting. It is a delay, not a ban: user ids are free, and a player who clears their browser's storage is a new user. `KICK_BY_ADDRESS=true` (off by default) makes new users from the kicked connection's address wait too: only an admin's kick makes such an entry, never for an address that could not be read, and it refuses only connections without a valid session token; everyone new behind that address waits with them. Nothing is stored: a restart forgets the list. It holds at most 4,096 user ids and 4,096 addresses: a kick past that lets the oldest back in before its minutes are over.
  - **Who may kick and mute.** `kick` and `mute` named any entity: with an app's id the rank comparison passed (an app has no rank), so any visitor could broadcast `mute` packets, with any value, as often as they liked. The target is now a player, the value a boolean, and the sender must outrank the target by the rank the players pane compares (the session's rank, a token's grant included, and the world's). In a world where everyone is an admin (no admin code) nobody outranks anybody: nobody kicks or mutes there, as the pane already showed.
  - **A mute covers the screen share.** A muted player's share is hidden on every client and shown again when the mute is lifted. The list of muted users holds at most 1,024 ids (at the limit the longest-muted absent player is unmuted, said to everyone); a muted player who comes back is said to be muted again, since a join snapshot made under size pressure leaves absent players' mutes out.
  - **Voice token.** It no longer allows data messages or a screen share's audio (nothing sends or plays either).
  - **What this does not do.** A mute and a voice level are carried out by the clients that listen. A muted player's client still sends its microphone to the voice server, and a modified client hears every voice in the world at any distance: the voice server sends every track to everyone. A modified client that was kicked can stay in the voice room and listen for as long as it stays connected, since the voice server keeps sending a connected client fresh tokens; six hours is only how long the token it was given when it joined lets it connect again. Nothing it sends is played, as it is nobody's player. The server makes no calls to the voice server. Any participant can show a screen share on any video node with a `screenId`; mute and kick are the tools against that.
- client: the world's image (a builder's setting, shown on every visitor's loading screen) and a blueprint's image in the Add pane were written into a stylesheet as an unquoted `url(...)`. A URL may hold `)`, `;` and `}`: one such URL ended the declaration and the rest of it was read as CSS, so whoever could set the image could style, hide or overlay the whole page for every visitor. The value is now a quoted, escaped `url("...")` for an http(s) or blob URL and `none` for anything else (`src/core/extras/cssImage.js`), set on the element's own style, where it can only be that one declaration's value. An image whose `url` was not a string also threw in the render.
- scripts: values a builder's script hands to host UI can no longer run code in visitors' browsers. React renders any object carrying the element `$$typeof` as markup, and `Symbol.for` works inside SES compartments, so a forged element with `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` in `control.setActions()` labels or `app.configure()` fields ran main-realm JavaScript on every desktop client.
  - **Actions.** `setActions` builds new `{ id, type, label, btn }` objects from strings (each property read once, length caps, at most 64 actions) and no longer writes `id` onto the script's objects.
  - **Fields.** `configure` copies fields through a per-type schema (`src/core/extras/appFields.js`); text becomes host strings, numbers must be numbers, `initial` and option values are JSON copies, unknown properties are dropped, and a button's `onClick` stays a function the sidebar calls but never renders.
  - **Everywhere else.** One helper (`src/core/extras/scriptText.js`, `toText`) turns any value bound for a React child into a host string or `''`: chat lines, the action HUD, field labels/hints/values, toasts, the hint box, blueprint and player names in the sidebar and apps list. Field components show a mistyped stored value (a string in a number field, an object in a color or file field) as empty or their default instead of throwing. Action and nametag labels, which are drawn every frame, must be primitive strings.
- server: the pages had no security headers at all. The world page asks for wallet signatures and sends tips, and any other site could show it in a frame under its own page and have a visitor click through it.
  - **Framing.** The pages (the world page, and the static pages on every answer, a 304 to a browser revalidating its copy included) say `Content-Security-Policy: frame-ancestors 'none'; base-uri 'none'; object-src 'none'; form-action 'self'` and `X-Frame-Options: DENY`: no other site can frame the page, and markup that got into it cannot change where relative URLs load from, embed a plugin or post a form to another site. Nothing on the pages uses what these forbid.
  - **A second copy of the world page.** `/index.html` was served as the built file, a working copy of the world page outside the route that serves `/`. Both names give the same page now.
  - **Uploaded scripts are text.** A `.js` file of the store was served from the page's own origin as `text/javascript`: its `sandbox` and download headers did not stop a `<script src>` or an import from running it with the page's rights, nor a worker in Chromium and WebKit (an engine that applies `sandbox` to a worker refused that one already). It is served as `text/plain` (the loaders read its text and evaluate it in a compartment, as before), which a browser with `nosniff` refuses for all three. With `ASSETS=s3` the type is stored with each object when the object is written: the built-in assets are written again at every start and get the new type, but a script uploaded before keeps `text/javascript` until its object's metadata is changed in the bucket or the object is replaced there (and a CDN in front may need invalidating). Uploading the same file again does not do it: the same bytes give the same name, and an object that exists is not written again.
  - **Every reply a route makes** says `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` (not a WebSocket upgrade, nor the errors the HTTP layer writes itself before any route runs: a 400 for a URL it cannot decode, a 408, a 431), and the pages say `Permissions-Policy: camera=(), geolocation=(), payment=(), usb=()`: in browsers that implement it (Firefox does not), the page and what it frames cannot use the camera, the location, the Payment Request API or a USB device. `payment` is that API only: wallet requests do not go through it. `Strict-Transport-Security` is sent only when `HSTS_MAX_AGE` (seconds) is set.
  - **What this is not.** It is not a policy on scripts, styles or connections: markup that got into the page could still run a script, and the page may connect to and load images from any host (app scripts and avatars name their own).
